Problem sheet 5 · Transport weights and pressure spaces

Locate the onset of a sign error and test which discrete pressure differences actually drive a velocity.

Resolve a layer

Let \(a=3\) m/s, \(\kappa=0.06\) m²/s on a 1 m interval. Find the minimum number of equal cells for the centred operator's nonpositive neighbour coefficients, and the number giving five cells per physical layer length. Estimate the upwind artificial diffusivity at each resolution.

Worked solution

The layer length is

\[\ell=\kappa/a=0.02\,\mathrm{m}.\]

The sign test requires \(h\le2\ell=0.04\) m, hence at least 25 cells. Five cells across \(\ell\) require \(h\le0.004\) m or 250 cells.

The upwind addition \(ah/2\) is 0.06 m²/s at 25 cells and 0.006 at 250. The monotonicity threshold and satisfactory physical resolution describe quite different demands.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Change transport direction

On an equally spaced grid take \(a<0\). Derive the upstream discrete derivative, its leading artificial diffusivity in \(a u'-\kappa u''\), and the boundary near which a thin outflow layer forms for fixed unequal Dirichlet values.

Worked solution

With flow toward decreasing \(x\), upstream information lies to the right, giving \((u_{j+1}-u_j)/h=u'_j+hu''_j/2+O(h^2)\).

Multiplying by a negative speed gives the modified coefficient \(-(\kappa+|a|h/2)u''\). On \([0,L]\) inflow is at \(L\) and outflow at zero. The term \(e^{ax/\kappa}\) decays away from zero, locating a possible outflow boundary layer there.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Find the pressure nullspace

For an even periodic grid with six pressure nodes, solve \(p_{j+1}-p_{j-1}=0\) for all \(j\). Remove the constant ambiguity and give the dimension of the remaining nullspace. Repeat the argument for five nodes.

Worked solution

The relation is

\[p_{j+2}=p_j.\]

Six nodes split into independent even and odd cycles, giving two arbitrary amplitudes. Removing the constant leaves one alternating mode.

With five nodes, increments of two cycle through every index, so all entries agree. Only the constant remains and zero mean removes it. This finite odd-grid observation by itself does not establish a mesh-independent mixed stability bound.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Chapter 11 · Multigrid and coarse-grid correction

Combine smoothing with a coarse residual equation and compute which components a two-grid correction can remove exactly.

From first principles

Derive the coarse-grid correction as a residual equation

Let a linear discretization satisfy

\[A_h u_h=b_h.\]

For an iterate \(\tilde u_h\) define error \(e_h=u_h-\tilde u_h\) and residual \(r_h=b_h-A_h\tilde u_h\). Subtracting the equations gives an exact identity:

\[A_he_h=r_h .\]

A coarse mesh cannot represent arbitrary fine error. Suppose the error remaining after smoothing is approximated by \(Pe_H\), with prolongation \(P\) from coarse to fine. Choose coarse coefficients so the remaining residual is orthogonal to the coarse test directions; with restriction \(R=P^\mathsf T\) this condition becomes

\[R(r_h-A_hPe_H)=0,\]
\[A_H e_H=Rr_h,\]
\[A_H=RA_hP .\]

Update the fine iterate by \(\tilde u_h\leftarrow\tilde u_h+Pe_H\). If the coarse solve is exact, this removes any error initially in the range of \(P\). Components outside that range must be reduced by smoothing or another correction. This separation explains why simply smoothing a fine mesh for longer can be inefficient when long-wavelength error survives each relaxation sweep.

Derivation and applications

Choose a Jacobi weight for the errors a coarse grid cannot resolve

A multigrid smoother should damp oscillations left invisible or ambiguous on the coarse mesh. Consider the uniform one-dimensional discretization of \(-u''\) with stencil \((-1,2,-1)/h^2\), away from boundaries or on a periodic grid. Let the error be a Fourier mode \(e_j=e^{ij\theta}\). The Jacobi diagonal is \(D=(2/h^2)I\). Inserting the Fourier mode into the discrete operator and dividing by \(D\) gives

\[(D^{-1}Ae)_j=\tfrac12(2-e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta})e_j=(1-\cos\theta)e_j.\]

One weighted Jacobi step \(\tilde u\leftarrow\tilde u+\omega D^{-1}(b-A\tilde u)\) therefore multiplies the error by a real factor. With standard factor-two coarsening, call the interval \(\pi/2\le|\theta|\le\pi\) high frequency:

\[g_\omega(\theta)=1-\omega(1-\cos\theta),\qquad g_\omega\in[1-2\omega,1-\omega]\quad(\omega>0).\]

The maximum absolute value on this interval lies at an endpoint. To minimize that worst factor, set the two endpoint values equal in magnitude and opposite in sign. The solution is

\[1-\omega=-(1-2\omega),\]
\[\omega=2/3,\]
\[\max_{\rm high}|g_\omega|=1/3 .\]

An ordinary undamped Jacobi sweep (\(\omega=1\)) leaves the checkerboard oscillating with unchanged magnitude. The optimal weight here depends on the operator, dimensionality and coarsening convention. It should not automatically be transferred to anisotropic grids or different stencils.

Worked example

Calculate how many smoothing sweeps are needed

A pure high-frequency error initially has amplitude \(0.900\) K. With \(\omega=2/3\), three sweeps reduce it to at most \(0.900/27=0.0333\) K. For the checkerboard the factor is \(-1/3\), so after the third sweep the error has reversed sign. Four sweeps bound the amplitude by \(0.0111\) K. A residual norm insensitive to sign still sees the decay.

Second application

Observe why smoothing alone is slow

At \(\theta=\pi/16\), the same smoother gives

\[g=1-(2/3)(1-\cos(\pi/16))=0.9872.\]

After ten sweeps a unit component still has amplitude \(0.9872^{10}=0.879\). A high-frequency component over the same ten sweeps is at most \(3^{-10}=1.69\times10^{-5}\). Coarse-grid correction targets the slowly decaying component that these sweeps leave behind.

Two kinds of persistent error

For a uniform Dirichlet grid let \(A_h\) be the positive three-point approximation to \(-u''\). A smooth algebraic error changes slowly between adjacent nodes. Its local residual can be modest even when its amplitude is appreciable. High-frequency errors have large second differences and respond rapidly to a suitable relaxation sweep. Weighted Jacobi uses \(u\leftarrow u+\omega D^{-1}(f-A_hu)\), with diagonal \(D=2I/h^2\). On a sine mode of grid angle \(\theta\) its error multiplier is \(1-\omega(1-\cos\theta)\).

For \(\omega=2/3\), this multiplier lies between \(-1/3\) and \(1/3\) for angles between \(\pi/2\) and \(\pi\). Low angles have multipliers near one, so repeated sweeps alone cost increasingly many iterations as the grid is refined. Those surviving smooth errors can often be represented on a coarser mesh. The purpose of restricting a residual is to solve an error equation for that representation.

Derive the correction

Let \(P\) interpolate coarse values to the fine grid and \(R\) restrict a fine vector. Choose the Galerkin coarse operator \(A_H=RA_hP\). Given current residual \(r=f-A_hu\), solve \(A_He_H=Rr\) and add \(Pe_H\). If the true error is precisely \(Pv\), the right-hand side equals \(A_Hv\). An exact coarse solve recovers \(v\) and removes the entire error in one correction. For general errors the new residual satisfies \(Rr_{\rm new}=0\); high-frequency components may remain on the fine grid.

Compute one correction by hand

Take \(h=1/4\) with three interior fine nodes and one interior coarse node at the midpoint. Linear interpolation is \(P=(1/2,1,1/2)^T\), and full weighting gives

\[R=P^T/2.\]

The fine operator is 16 times the tridiagonal matrix with diagonal 2 and off-diagonal −1. Multiplication gives \(A_hP=(0,16,0)^T\) and \(A_H=8\). Suppose \(u=0\) and the exact fine solution is \(P\), so the load is \((0,16,0)^T\). The restricted residual is 8. The coarse correction returns the coefficient one and removes the error exactly.

For error \(e=(1,0,-1)^T\), the fine residual is \(32e\), whose restriction is zero. The coarse grid cannot correct this component. Its grid angle is \(\pi/2\), so one weighted Jacobi sweep leaves \(e/3\). These two vectors demonstrate why combining the two operations matters. Repeating the idea recursively gives a V-cycle. With a fixed number of smoothing steps per level, geometrically shrinking grids have total work proportional to the finest number of unknowns, provided coarse solvers and transfers also have linear cost. Good convergence rates still depend on smoothing and approximation properties for the actual operator.

Coarse linear solves do not have to be exact at every level

In the three-node example with fine error \(P\), suppose an approximate coarse solve returns 0.9 instead of one. The correction leaves \(0.1P\), so the restricted residual falls from 8 to 0.8. A recursive V-cycle effectively uses one approximate correction of this kind on intermediate levels; only the smallest system is usually solved directly. Measure the residual reduction per cycle across a family of grids. A roughly constant work per unknown is valuable only if the number of cycles required for a fixed reduction also remains controlled.

Predict a smoothing failure in anisotropic diffusion

For the operator \(-\alpha u_{xx}-\beta u_{yy}\) on an equal-spacing Cartesian grid, weighted point Jacobi has error multiplier

\[g(\theta_x,\theta_y)=1-\omega\frac{\alpha(1-\cos\theta_x)+\beta(1-\cos\theta_y)}{\alpha+\beta}.\]

This follows by substituting a Fourier error mode into the tridiagonal second differences in each direction and dividing by the diagonal \(2(\alpha+\beta)/h^2\). Algebraic smoothness depends on the coefficients, while representability on a coarse mesh depends on geometric sampling. Under strong anisotropy a mode highly oscillatory in the weak direction can produce little residual relative to the diagonal and therefore survive point relaxation. A standard coarse interpolation space may also fail to represent it.

A line smoother solves all strongly coupled unknowns along a row together. Its block diagonal retains the horizontal second differences and the diagonal part of the vertical coupling. Modes constant along that row see no horizontal stiffness after applying the block, so the scale of their correction is set by the weaker vertical coupling rather than the large point diagonal. Alternatively, semicoarsening retains fine resolution in a direction in which troublesome oscillations must remain representable. The correct choice depends on the orientation of the anisotropy relative to the actual mesh.

Evaluate an error mode ignored by the ordinary two-grid cycle

Take a periodic grid with an even number of rows, \(\alpha=100,\ \beta=1,\ \omega=2/3\), and error \(e_{ij}=(-1)^j\), constant along each row. Its discrete horizontal curvature is zero and its vertical curvature has eigenvalue \(4/h^2\). Point Jacobi divides by \(202/h^2\), giving \(g=1-4/(303)=299/303\approx0.9868\). Ten sweeps still leave about \(0.876\) of the error amplitude. Geometrically the mode is as oscillatory as the grid permits, yet pointwise smoothing barely changes it.

Full weighting in the vertical direction uses weights \(1/4,1/2,1/4\). Applied to the alternating signs it gives zero, so the restricted residual also vanishes and an ideal coarse solve receives no information about this error. This calculation identifies a concrete failure of the usual assumption that all components missed by coarsening have already been strongly damped. A high-quality coarse linear solve cannot repair a zero coarse right-hand side.

For a horizontal line Jacobi block the horizontal part contributes zero on a row-constant vector, leaving block eigenvalue \(2/h^2\). The preconditioned error-mode eigenvalue is now \((4/h^2)/(2/h^2)=2\), and a damped line sweep has multiplier \(1-(2/3)2=-1/3\). Ten such sweeps reduce the amplitude to \(1/59049\). Each line sweep costs more than a point sweep, so the practical comparison also depends on implementation and geometry. The dramatic contrast in this mode calculation explains which coupling the extra solve is buying and why iteration counts alone are insufficient for benchmarking.

For diagonal anisotropy the identity of a strongly coupled row can change as the material rotates across the grid. A line relaxation designed only for horizontal coupling may then lose its advantage in another subdomain. Compare mode or patch tests aligned with several local principal directions before generalising the success of the horizontal example.

Chapter 12 · Adjoints and errors in a chosen output

Weight equation residuals by the response of a chosen observation and distinguish algebraic error from mesh error.

From first principles

Derive an output error from an adjoint solve

Consider a nonsingular discrete linear problem \(Au=b\) with approximate solution \(\tilde u\). A user may care about one output \(J(u)=c^\mathsf Tu\) rather than every entry of \(u\). The unknown error \(e=u-\tilde u\) satisfies \(Ae=r\) with residual \(r=b-A\tilde u\). Directly computing \(e\) would require another solve for a complete field. To weight the known residual for this output, select a vector \(z\) whose transposed action reproduces the output coefficients:

\[A^\mathsf Tz=c,\]
\[c^\mathsf T e=z^\mathsf T A e .\]

Substitution yields an exact algebraic output identity and a norm bound:

\[J(u)-J(\tilde u)=z^\mathsf T r,\qquad |J(u)-J(\tilde u)|\le\|z\|_2\|r\|_2 .\]

Large entries of \(z\) identify rows where a residual matters strongly to this particular observable, including possible sign cancellation between rows. A different output requires a different adjoint. The identity concerns error in solving the stated algebraic system. To estimate error relative to a continuum solution, discretization residuals and a suitable continuous or enriched adjoint must also enter.

Derivation and applications

Weight a distributed residual for one point-temperature measurement

A local output need not be equally sensitive to residuals everywhere on the domain. Consider \(-u''=f\) on \(0<x<L\) with homogeneous Dirichlet boundaries and constant unit diffusivity. Suppose a smooth reconstructed numerical field \(\tilde u\) satisfies both boundary values. Define the residual \(r=f+\tilde u''\) so that the error \(e=u-\tilde u\) obeys \(-e''=r\). Seek the error in one output \(J(u)=u(\xi)\) at an interior location \(\xi\).

Introduce a weight \(z\) that solves \(-z''=\delta(x-\xi)\) with zero end values. Away from \(\xi\) the weight is linear, and integrating the equation through the observation point determines its slope jump:

\[z(0)=z(L)=0,\]
\[z'(\xi^-)-z'(\xi^+)=1.\]

Continuity at \(\xi\) and the endpoint conditions determine both linear pieces. They give an explicit tent-shaped influence function

\[z(x)=\begin{cases}x(L-\xi)/L,&x\le\xi,\\ \xi(L-x)/L,&x\ge\xi.\end{cases}\]

Multiply the error equation by \(z\) and integrate by parts on the two intervals. The exterior terms vanish and the unit slope jump isolates the requested point value:

\[J(u)-J(\tilde u)=e(\xi)=\int_0^L z(x)\,r(x)\,dx .\]

A residual near the chosen output can have a larger effect than an equal residual near a prescribed boundary. Residuals of opposite signs can cancel for this observation without producing a globally accurate field. For piecewise linear finite-element fields, the distributional second derivative also contains jumps of the first derivative; omitting those terms would invalidate the identity.

Worked example

Calculate a local temperature error

Take \(L=1\) m and \(\xi=0.250\) m. Suppose \(r=8.00\) K/m² on \(0<x<0.250\) m and zero elsewhere. The relevant weight is \(0.750x\), so the point error is

\[8(0.750)(0.250)^2/2=0.1875\,\mathrm{K}.\]

The positive residual means the exact temperature at the observation exceeds the reconstructed value.

Second application

Move the same residual nearer the far boundary

Put the same \(8.00\) K/m² on \(0.750<x<1.00\) m instead. Now the weight is \(0.250(1-x)\), yielding \(8(0.250)(0.250)^2/2=0.0625\) K. Both residuals have identical \(L^1\) and \(L^2\) magnitude; the first affects this particular sensor three times as strongly. Moving the sensor would change the ranking without changing either residual field.

A residual has a direction

Let \(Au=b\) be an invertible linear discrete model and let \(Q(u)=c^Tu\) report a scalar output, for example a weighted mean temperature. For a computed approximation \(\tilde u\), set \(r=b-A\tilde u\). The error is \(A^{-1}r\), and hence \(Q(u)-Q(\tilde u)=c^TA^{-1}r\). Define the adjoint or dual solution by \(A^Tz=c\). Then the exact output identity is

\[Q(u)-Q(\tilde u)=z^Tr.\]

A single adjoint gives the output effect of many possible residuals. A large residual where the observation has low sensitivity can contribute less than a small residual elsewhere. Opposite contributions can cancel in this particular output, even though they represent substantial state errors.

Compute the weight of each row

Take \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), \(b=(1,0)^T\), and observe the second unknown with \(c=(0,1)^T\). Then \(u=(2/3,1/3)^T\) and \(z=(1/3,2/3)^T\). For \(\tilde u=(0.6,0.3)^T\), the residual is \((0.1,0)^T\). Its weighted sum is \(1/30\), exactly the missing part \(1/3-0.3\). If a solve produced residual \((0,0.1)^T\) instead, the same residual norm would double the output error. This output cares twice as much about the second row as about the first.

For residual \((0.2,-0.1)^T\), the signed estimate is exactly zero. The sum of absolute contributions is \(2/15\), an upper bound insensitive to cancellation. An analyst interested only in the selected output can accept a calculation whose state vector another observer would reject. Specify the observation before choosing stopping and refinement criteria.

Move from an algebraic error to a mesh error

An accurately solved finite-element system has almost zero algebraic residual in its own discrete test space. It can still differ from the continuum solution. Let \(a(u,v)=\ell(v)\) be the continuum weak equation and define \(R(v)=\ell(v)-a(u_h,v)\). For a linear observation the continuum dual satisfies \(a(v,z)=Q(v)\), giving \(Q(u)-Q(u_h)=R(z)\). Galerkin orthogonality means \(R(z_h)=0\) for every test function on the same mesh, hence \(R(z)=R(z-z_h)\).

Estimating this term requires a dual approximation richer than the test space used for the primal solve, or an independent reconstruction. Integrating element residuals by parts can expose both cell defects and jumps in normal flux. Weight these local contributions by the unresolved dual variation to discover which regions can affect the output strongly. Check an estimated correction against actual output changes on a refined mesh; a poor dual approximation can misidentify important regions even when the primal residuals have been evaluated exactly.

Compare a point observation with an average

In the two-unknown example, replace the second-node observation by the mean \(c=(1/2,1/2)^T\). The constant vector is an eigenvector of \(A\) with eigenvalue one, so the adjoint becomes \(z=c\). A purely antisymmetric residual \((0.1,-0.1)^T\) makes the mean output error zero. For the earlier second-node output the same residual gives \(-1/30\). A mesh designed to resolve an average can therefore allocate effort differently from one designed for a single sensor. Whenever a reported error changes markedly between observables, inspect the dual weights before attributing the change to a flaw in the physical simulation.

Turn primal and dual approximation errors into an output bound

For a symmetric coercive bilinear form \(a\), let the exact state \(u\) satisfy \(a(u,v)=\ell(v)\), and the exact dual \(z\) satisfy \(a(v,z)=Q(v)\) for a linear output \(Q\). With conforming Galerkin approximations on the same trial space, \(a(u-u_h,v_h)=0\) for every discrete test. Consequently \(Q(u)-Q(u_h)=a(u-u_h,z-z_h)\). Cauchy–Schwarz in the energy inner product gives an absolute bound by \(\|u-u_h\|_a\|z-z_h\|_a\). Accuracy of either the state or the observation sensitivity can separately make the output error small.

When the primal and dual energy errors both decrease proportionally to \(h\), their product guarantees an \(O(h^2)\) output error under these assumptions. It does not guarantee second order for every possible observation. A point evaluation in a higher-dimensional continuum problem may be unbounded on the energy space or yield a dual solution with a strong singularity. Then the dual error may converge much more slowly. Likewise nonconforming approximation, inexact integration or inconsistent boundary terms introduce additional residual terms that are absent from the simple Galerkin cancellation.

A case where the product bound is exact

Let \(a(v,w)=v^TAw\) with \(A=\operatorname{diag}(2,8)\), exact state \(u=(1,2)^T\), and reduced approximation space spanned by \(s=(1,1)^T\). The Galerkin coefficient is

\[(s^TAu)/(s^TAs)=(2+16)/10=1.8.\]

Choose output \(Q(v)=(6,8)v\); its exact dual is \(z=(3,1)^T\) since \(Az=(6,8)^T\). Projecting that dual onto the same space gives coefficient \((6+8)/10=1.4\). The primal and dual errors are therefore \((-0.8,0.2)^T\) and \((1.6,-0.4)^T\).

Their energy inner product is

\[2(-0.8)(1.6)+8(0.2)(-0.4)=-3.2.\]

Direct evaluation gives \(Q(u)=22\) and \(Q(u_h)=25.2\), confirming the signed error exactly. Squared energy errors are

\[2(0.8)^2+8(0.2)^2=1.6,\]
\[2(1.6)^2+8(0.4)^2=6.4.\]

Their square-root product is 3.2, attaining the absolute bound because the two error vectors are antiparallel. The bound cannot determine the sign; the signed dual residual can.

Replace the observation by \(Q_2(v)=(2,8)v\). Its exact dual is \(s\), already representable, so \(z-z_h=0\) and the output error vanishes even though the state error is unchanged. Indeed \(Q_2(u)=18=Q_2(u_h)\). A mesh judged solely by that particular observable could look exact. A different sensor weighing the same simulation states produces a demonstrably nonzero output error, which is why adaptive refinement objectives should name their actual observations.

If the approximate dual used the same one-dimensional space and one simply evaluated the primal residual on it, the reported correction would be exactly zero for either output. The first observation shows that such a number can be badly misleading. An enriched dual must include a direction outside the primal test space; in this two-coordinate example, adding either coordinate direction gives the full space and recovers the signed error exactly. On a fine physical mesh full enrichment may be too expensive, making local higher-order reconstruction or an independently refined dual mesh attractive. The product bound then clarifies which additional error in the dual approximation needs to be controlled.

It also identifies the observation for which refining the primal mesh alone is unnecessary.

Bound the uncertainty of an approximate dual correction

A practical enriched dual \(\tilde z\) may itself have numerical error. In the symmetric positive definite algebraic setting the difference between true output error and its estimated correction is \((z-\tilde z)^Tr\), where \(r=b-Au_h\). With dual residual \(r_z=c-A\tilde z\), this equals \(r_z^TA^{-1}r\). Cauchy–Schwarz bounds its magnitude by \(\|r_z\|_{A^{-1}}\|r\|_{A^{-1}}\). This identity explains why a small ordinary residual norm can be insufficient for either the primal or dual solve: directions associated with small eigenvalues receive greater weight in the inverse-energy norm.

In the preceding example choose \(\tilde z=(2.5,1)^T\). The primal residual is \((-1.6,1.6)^T\); the approximate correction is therefore \(-4+1.6=-2.4\), moving the coarse output 25.2 to 22.8. The actual output is 22, leaving error −0.8 after correction. The dual residual is \((1,0)^T\), with squared inverse-energy norm \(1/2\). The squared primal residual norm in the same metric is 1.6. Their product of norms is \(\sqrt{0.8}\approx0.8944\), containing the observed remaining discrepancy. For larger problems, estimating these norms can itself require a separate bound or preconditioner. Reporting only the corrected value without assessing the accuracy of the dual can make a visually improved output appear more certain than the calculation warrants.

Which source errors matter to a point measurement?

For \(-u''=f\) with zero values at both ends of the unit interval, seek the sensitivity of \(u(a)\) to a point load. The adjoint is piecewise linear, vanishes at the endpoints, is continuous at \(a\) and has derivative jump minus one.

Problem sheet 6 · Transfers and observation error

Track which error can survive a grid transfer and compute an exact adjoint correction for a nonsymmetric system.

A smooth mode survives smoothing

Weighted Jacobi for a Dirichlet Laplacian uses \(\omega=2/3\). Compute the multiplier at grid angles \(\pi/3\) and \(2\pi/3\), and the error left by two sweeps. Explain how this affects the choice of a coarse-grid space.

Worked solution

For \(\pi/3\) the multiplier is \(1-(2/3)(1-1/2)=2/3\), leaving \(4/9\) after two sweeps. At \(2\pi/3\) it is \(1-(2/3)(1+1/2)=0\), so the error vanishes in one sweep.

Smoother errors can persist while oscillatory components decay. A coarse interpolation space should approximate those persistent smooth components. A coarse solve alone cannot remove arbitrary fine-grid error.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Test exact coarse correction

Use \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), \(P=(1,1)^T\), \(R=P^T/2\). Find the coarse operator and the coarse correction for true error \((3,1)^T\). Verify the restricted residual afterward.

Worked solution

\(AP=(1,1)^T\) and \(RAP=1\). The initial residual is

\[A(3,1)^T=(5,-1)^T.\]

Its restriction is 2, giving coarse correction \(P\,2=(2,2)^T\).

The remaining error is \((1,-1)^T\), with residual \((3,-3)^T\). Restricting gives zero even though the fine error remains. An exact coarse solve eliminated only the represented component.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Use the transpose

Let \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\0&3\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), \(c=(1,0)^T\), and suppose a computed state's residual is

\[r=(0,0.3)^T.\]

Compute the observation error with an adjoint, verify it by solving \(Ae=r\), and explain the sign.

Worked solution

Solving \(A^Tz=c\) gives \(2z_1=1,\ z_1+3z_2=0\), so \(z=(1/2,-1/6)^T\). Its dot product with \(r\) is −0.05.

The error equation gives \(e_2=0.1,\ 2e_1+0.1=0\), hence

\[c^Te=-0.05.\]

A positive second-row defect forces a negative first-component change through the positive off-diagonal coupling. Using \(A\) instead of its transpose for the dual would miss that effect.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Inspect the Taylor remainder with the correct and wrong-sign adjoint. The explorer uses a two-state equilibrium; apply the stated scalar or nonsymmetric operator to obtain this exercise’s exact gradient.

Decide if another grid refinement answers the engineering question

A steady diffusion solver predicts a dimensionless mean sensor value of 0.42800, 0.42200 and 0.42050 on meshes with 16, 32 and 64 cells per side. The linear solver reports relative residuals below 10⁻¹⁰. Estimate the converged value and the error on the finest grid. A measurement reads 0.4170 with uncertainty ±0.0010. Can the discrepancy be attributed to mesh error alone?

Worked solution

Assume the same smooth solution, geometry, sensor integration and refinement ratio on all three grids. The ratio of successive differences is 0.0060/0.0015 = 4.0, giving observed order p = 2. Extrapolate the fine estimate by adding (0.42050−0.42200)/(2²−1) = −0.00050. The resulting estimate is 0.42000.

The fine-grid spatial error magnitude is therefore about 0.00050. A relative algebraic residual near 10⁻¹⁰ describes the discrete equations and is in different units from the absolute sensor error. It cannot replace this grid calculation. Repeating the study with a local peak instead of a mean can give a different observed order.

The measurement interval spans 0.4160 to 0.4180. Even the extrapolated prediction exceeds its upper end by 0.0020. Under the assumed error model mesh refinement alone appears too small to explain that gap. Test coefficient uncertainty, boundary data and sensor averaging separately. The numerical experiment in chapter 6 supplies a known exact field for checking the discretisation independently of the physical measurement.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Refine the grid on a known solution and keep the small linear-solver residual separate from the spatial field error.

Chapter 13 · Pure flux boundaries, compatibility and the missing constant

If a steady diffusion equation prescribes only fluxes, a solver can return a singular-matrix warning for two different physical reasons. Derive the mass balance and the nullspace separately, then decide when choosing a reference value resolves the problem.

Test the continuum equation with a constant

On \(0<x<1\) take a unit conductivity, positive rightward flux \(q=-u'\), and a steady source f with \(q'=f\). Prescribe the rightward flux at both endpoints as \(q_L,q_R\). Integrating the divergence equation gives

\[q_R-q_L=\int_0^1 f\,dx.\]

The outward unit normal is negative at the left endpoint, so the net outward flow is \(q_R-q_L\), even when both recorded sensor values are positive. In several dimensions the same requirement follows by integrating the divergence and applying the divergence theorem.

\[\int_0^1 u'v'\,dx=\int_0^1 fv\,dx+q_L v(0)-q_R v(1).\]

Testing the weak equation with \(v=1\) makes its left side identically zero. This recovers the flux balance without differentiating an approximate field. For every compatible right-hand side, adding any constant to u leaves its gradient and both prescribed fluxes unchanged. The magnitude of a potential is undetermined, while potential differences and flux can be well defined. Each disconnected region with pure-flux boundaries has its own compatibility equation and arbitrary constant.

Background · Compatibility condition
\[\int_\Omega f\,dV=-\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_nu\,dA\]

For minus the Laplacian of u equal to f with pure Neumann data, this integral condition is required. A constant still has to be fixed separately.

Separate the left nullspace test from the gauge condition

Take cell-centred finite volumes with equal width h and face flux \(q_{i+1/2}=-(u_{i+1}-u_i)/h\). The balance of each cell is \(q_{i+1/2}-q_{i-1/2}=h f_i\), with prescribed values replacing the two exterior faces. Add all rows. Interior face contributions cancel in equal and opposite pairs. The resulting symmetric matrix A has a constant right null vector and its range is orthogonal to that vector. Let b be the integrated loads including boundary terms and \(w_i=h\) the integration weights. A mean value \(\bar u\) on this unit interval can be imposed by the augmented system

\[\begin{bmatrix}A&w\\ w^T&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}u\\ \lambda\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}b\\\bar u\end{bmatrix},\qquad \lambda={\mathbf 1^T b\over \mathbf 1^Tw}.\]

For compatible data \(\lambda=0\). For incompatible data the first block solves for a deliberately modified source \(f_i-\lambda\). Choosing a mean makes the augmented system solvable either way; checking the multiplier identifies what it solved. Pinning a node and silently dropping one conservation equation can conceal the same mismatch as an artificial reaction at the pin.

Predict four cell values and audit an inconsistent measurement

Set \(f=2,\ q_L=0,\ q_R=2\). Exact integration gives

\[q=2x,\ u=-x^2+C.\]

For a continuous mean of zero \(C=1/3\). With four equal cells the centre coordinates are 1/8, 3/8, 5/8 and 7/8. The centroid samples of \(-x^2\) have mean −0.328125, so the discrete mean-zero solution is \(0.3125,0.1875,-0.0625,-0.4375\). Adjacent differences give interior face fluxes 0.5, 1.0 and 1.5. The difference between the discrete centre values and the continuous mean-zero samples is the constant \(-h^2/12\); every interior face gradient is exact.

Now change only the recorded right flux to 2.4. The original source supplies 2 units per unit time and the boundaries demand 2.4. Thus \(\lambda=-0.40\) and the augmented system supplies an effective uniform source of 2.4. Alternatively, keeping the original source and the left measurement forces a right flux of 2. The plot shows both reconstructions and tells which data were modified. Adjusting only the mean translates either field without changing its slope. In a transient storage model, imbalance would change the mean in time instead of permitting a steady solution.

Set a nonzero flux mismatch, then vary the mean and the cell count independently. Observe which differences can be removed by a vertical translation. Check that the integrated imbalance remains the same under refinement.

Tutorial · locate the bias and choose a comparison observable

A unit-length domain receives \(f(x)=1+2x\). A left sensor reports rightward flux 0.3 and a right sensor reports 2.1. Compute the integrated residual. If both flux sensors are trusted, find the constant source adjustment implied by the augmented system. If only the source and left sensor are trusted, predict the right measurement. What quantity can two solvers compare without agreeing on a gauge?

Show the diagnostic

The source integral is 2 and net outflow is

\[2.1-0.3=1.8.\]

Their difference is +0.2. The multiplier is +0.2, so the adjusted source would be \(0.8+2x\). Keeping the original source instead predicts

\[q_R=0.3+2=2.3.\]

Differences \(u(x_b)-u(x_a)\), gradients and fluxes are gauge independent. A single imbalance equation cannot tell which instrument is wrong. Independent source or flux measurements are needed to choose between these reconstructions.

Chapter 14 · Building a local residual estimator and refining where it matters

A finite-element solution can satisfy the assembled equations to machine precision while still missing curvature between nodes. Integrate the residual of the computed field by parts on each element and use the resulting cell and interface terms to decide where to spend the next degrees of freedom.

Ask what the discrete solution does to an unresolved test function

Consider \(-u''=f\) on a unit interval with zero Dirichlet data. Use continuous piecewise-linear finite elements with exact integration on a possibly nonuniform mesh. The weak solution satisfies \(a(u,v)=\int u'v'=\int fv\) for every test function vanishing at the endpoints. For \(e=u-u_h\), subtract the computed field to obtain

\[a(e,v)=R(v)=\int fv-\int u_h'v'.\]

The algebraic system makes \(R(v_h)=0\) for all functions in the chosen finite-element space. Tests with more local structure expose what is missing.

\[R(v)=\sum_K\int_K (f+u_h'')v\,dx+\sum_{i\ {\rm interior}} J_i v(x_i),\]
\[J_i=u_h'(x_i^+)-u_h'(x_i^-).\]

The sign follows by adding the right boundary term of the left cell and the left boundary term of the right cell. On a linear cell \(u_h''=0\) classically. Its piecewise constant gradient jumps at interior nodes. In diffusion with varying coefficient k the corresponding jump is in \(k u_h'\), since continuity of normal flux is the conservation condition. A discontinuous material coefficient can cause an exact gradient jump without any flux imbalance.

Scale the cell and node terms before marking

Subtract the finite-element interpolant or a local quasi-interpolant from v inside the residual. Galerkin orthogonality permits this subtraction and bounds the remaining test magnitude by local length times its gradient. This leads to a computable residual indicator. One common one-dimensional choice with a symmetric edge length \(h_i\) equal here to the smaller neighbouring cell is

\[\eta_K^2=h_K^2\int_K f^2\,dx+{1\over2}\sum_{i\in\partial K\ {\rm interior}}h_i J_i^2,\]
\[\eta^2=\sum_K\eta_K^2.\]

Sharing the node contribution prevents double counting. Under the usual mesh and coefficient assumptions such estimates bound the energy error up to constants; their absolute numbers need not equal the measured error. Data oscillation can matter if the source is approximated or oscillates within a cell. To mark by bulk contribution, sort \(\eta_K^2\) in descending order and take cells until their sum exceeds a chosen fraction of the global squared estimator. Bisect marked cells, transfer the coefficient data, assemble and solve again. Reusing the old gradient jumps after refinement would skip the effect of the new trial space.

Work through the first two-element decision

Let \(f=1\) on the left half and \(f=9\) on the right. With two equal cells and zero end values there is a single interior hat unknown. Diagonal stiffness is 4 and the integrated hat load is

\[1(0.25)+9(0.25)=2.5.\]

The middle value is therefore 0.625. The slopes are 1.25 and −1.25, giving \(J=-2.5\). Since \(h=0.5\), the two cell terms are 0.125 and 10.125. Each side receives \((1/2)(0.5)(2.5)^2=1.5625\) from the node. The right cell contributes \(11.6875/(1.6875+11.6875)=87.38\%\) of the squared indicator. A 50% bulk mark selects only that cell.

For this particular problem the finite-element solution equals the piecewise-linear interpolant of the true nodal values. This follows by testing the derivative of the interpolation error against a constant basis gradient on each element. For constant f on each element the derivative error is therefore a line of slope −f with zero mean. Its exact squared energy norm is \(\sum f_K^2 h_K^3/12\). Splitting the right half into two equal cells reduces that half's contribution by a factor of four. Total energy error falls from 0.924 to 0.470 with one extra degree of freedom. Splitting only the left cell leaves about 0.920. Run the loop and compare the indicator's ranking with this independent verification quantity.

Run the mesh from zero to three refinement rounds with a 50% bulk mark. Record error against free values. Repeat with a 90% mark and compare equal computational costs where available. Which choice targets the source localization most strongly?

Tutorial · predict the benefit of two candidate splits

Consider a left cell of length 0.50 with \(f=1\) and a right cell of length 0.125 with \(f=9\). Which single split would remove more exact squared energy error? Show how stiffness residual and source localization answer distinct questions.

Show the local comparison

Bisection removes three quarters of that cell's \(f^2h^3/12\). Before division by 12 the left weight is \(1(0.5)^3=0.125\); the right weight is

\[81(0.125)^3=0.1582.\]

Refining the small right cell still yields about 27% more improvement. Conversely the assembled nodal residual could already be zero on either mesh after a direct solve. Reducing the algebraic tolerance further would improve neither piecewise-linear profile. This calculation is a verification benchmark made possible by the manufactured piecewise-constant data.

Chapter 15 · What a periodic grid does to a decaying Fourier mode

A periodic diffusion solver predicts the shape of a sampled wave while its amplitude decays too slowly. Fourier analysis separates spatial differentiation error, an unresolved physical mode and errors caused by the time integrator.

Diagonalise the differential operator on the periodic interval

Use a dimensionless coordinate \(0\le x<2\pi\) and solve \(u_t=\kappa u_{xx}\) with periodic values and periodic derivatives. The complex exponentials \(e^{imx}\), with integer m, satisfy both conditions and are eigenfunctions of the second derivative. Put \(u(x,t)=A_m(t)e^{imx}\) and cancel the common exponential. The scalar amplitude equation is

\[A'_m=-\kappa m^2 A_m.\]

Thus every cosine or sine mode decays by \(\exp(-\kappa m^2t)\), with no phase shift, while the m=0 mean is conserved.

On a uniform grid \(x_j=2\pi j/N\), the samples of \(m\) and \(m+\ell N\) agree for every integer \(\ell\). A discrete Fourier transform returns coefficients in one representative band. For the real cosine test with even N we can fold a positive physical mode into \(k=\min(r,N-r)\), \(r=m\bmod N\). A Fourier spectral Laplacian multiplies that coefficient by \(-k^2\). For a resolved mode it differentiates the trigonometric interpolant exactly, up to arithmetic. For unresolved data it differentiates the aliased representative; no operation using only those samples can identify the original m.

Apply the centred difference stencil to a single wave

The standard second difference is \((u_{j+1}-2u_j+u_{j-1})/h^2\), \(h=2\pi/N\). Put the grid wave \(u_j=e^{ikjh}\) into the stencil. Factor out the common phase and simplify with \(e^{i\xi}+e^{-i\xi}=2\cos\xi\). This gives the symbol and its small-h expansion:

\[{e^{ikh}-2+e^{-ikh}\over h^2}=-{4\over h^2}\sin^2(kh/2)=-k^2+{k^4h^2\over12}+O(k^6h^4).\]

The effective squared wavenumber is smaller than \(k^2\) for every nonconstant mode in the resolved band, so this spatial approximation decays too slowly if the time equation is integrated exactly. The error is modest when \(kh\ll1\), but at the Nyquist mode \(kh=\pi\) the ratio is

\[4/\pi^2=0.4053.\]

A stable time integrator cannot repair this spatial attenuation error without additional modeling. The experiment exponentiates both discretizations' symbols exactly, isolating space and sampling here.

Measure separate errors with the same sampled initial data

Choose \(\kappa=0.5,\ m=3,\ N=16\). The true squared wavenumber is 9 and the difference stencil gives 8.006. Starting at unit amplitude, at \(t=0.20\) exact and spectral amplitudes are 0.40657; finite differences give 0.44906. Doubling N to 32 changes the difference result to 0.41716. The absolute amplitude error falls from 0.04249 to 0.01059, a ratio of 4.01 consistent with the second-order expansion. This is a spatial-convergence study because time evolution introduces no truncation error.

Now take \(m=12,\ N=16\). Every sampled cosine equals the low mode \(k=4\) at \(t=0\). At \(t=0.02\), the continuum amplitude for the true physical mode is \(e^{-1.44}=0.237\), whereas the spectral grid predicts

\[e^{-0.16}=0.852.\]

A spectral computation can therefore agree exactly with its trigonometric interpolant and disagree strongly with the evolution of the intended signal. Sampling on a finer grid or obtaining prior bandwidth information resolves that ambiguity. The mean mode is particularly revealing when a nonconstant physical cosine aliases to a constant.

Start at mode 3 and successively double N from 8 to 32. Compute two amplitude-error ratios at the same time. Then use mode 12 on 16 points to observe an alias, and increase N until the Fourier grid represents the intended mode.

Tutorial · resolve a damping rate to a target tolerance

For a mode well inside the grid bandwidth, estimate the number of samples per wavelength needed for a relative error below 1% in the decay rate with centred differences. Test the estimate for \(m=2\). How could the time integrator contaminate a measurement of this spatial error?

Show the resolution estimate

Writing p for samples per wavelength gives

\[kh=2\pi/p.\]

The relative rate error is approximately \((kh)^2/12\). Requiring at most 0.01 gives \(p\gtrsim18.14\); try 19 samples per wavelength, or \(N=38\) for \(m=2\). The exact ratio \([\sin(\pi/19)/(\pi/19)]^2\) is about 0.991. A forward-Euler step would replace exponential propagation by \(1-\kappa k_{\rm eff}^2\Delta t\). Its repeated-step error could partly mask the underdamping of the spatial stencil. Compare with the exponential of the discrete symbol before attributing a measured rate to space alone.

A spatial sine also diagonalises the five-point Dirichlet Laplacian on a square. Use the same modified wave-number argument in both coordinate directions. Run python poisson-mode.py --mode 2 --cells 8 16 32 and compare the observed nodal error with the discrete eigenvalue prediction in the CSV. This is a steady forced problem whose spatial operator can be checked independently of any time integrator. Download the OneSimulation verification model.

Chapter 16 · Why two finite-element wave solvers propagate the same mode at different speeds

Build the element mass matrix by integrating shape functions and track the effect of diagonal lumping on the wave spectrum.

From a continuum wave to nodal masses

For \(u_{tt}=c^2u_{xx}\), multiply by a test function and integrate by parts. On a periodic line the boundary contributions cancel. A piecewise-linear element of width \(h\) uses shape functions \(1-\xi,\xi\), with \(\xi\in[0,1]\). Integrating products of shape functions gives

\[M_e=(h/6)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right).\]

Integrating derivative products gives

\[K_e=(c^2/h)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right).\]

Row-sum lumping replaces each mass row by its sum on the corresponding diagonal, \(h/2\). This preserves the mass associated with a uniform velocity but changes the response to a velocity that varies within an element.

Background · Numerical dispersion
\[c_{\rm ph,h}=\omega_h(k)/k\]

A discretised wave can retain its amplitude and still propagate at the wrong speed. Compare phase and group errors over the actual resolved wavelengths.

Diagonalise the assembled periodic problem

A periodic nodal mode has \(u_j=e^{ij\theta}\). Each neighbour contributes \(e^{\pm i\theta}\) relative to node \(j\). Thus \(K\) multiplies by \(2c^2(1-\cos\theta)/h\), consistent \(M\) by \(h(2+\cos\theta)/3\), and lumped \(M\) by \(h\). Dividing stiffness by mass gives the discrete squared frequency. Relative to the exact frequency \(c\theta/h\),

\[\frac{\omega_L}{c\theta/h}=\frac{2\sin(\theta/2)}{\theta},\]
\[\frac{\omega_C}{c\theta/h}=\frac{2\sin(\theta/2)}{\theta}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2+\cos\theta}}.\]

Both tend to one for long waves; the leading quadratic errors have opposite signs. This is a spatial effect, present even with exact time integration.

Two wavelengths in one mesh

At four cells per wavelength \(\theta=\pi/2\). The lumped ratio is \(2\sqrt2/\pi\approx0.9003\); the consistent ratio is about 1.1027. One wave solver lags by roughly ten per cent and the other leads by roughly ten per cent. At eight cells per wavelength the ratios move much closer to one, but repeated propagation over many wavelengths can still accumulate visible phase error.

At the shortest grid mode \(\theta=\pi\), the consistent frequency is \(\sqrt{12}c/h\) and the lumped frequency \(2c/h\). A central-difference time integrator stable for \(h_t\omega\le2\) therefore requires a smaller step for the consistent mass. Accuracy at one wavelength and stability at the grid scale are different design criteria.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on interpreting a convergence test

Which mass gives the exact kinetic energy for a linear velocity varying from \(v\) to \(-v\) on one element?

Worked solution

The continuum energy with unit density is \(hv^2/6\). The consistent matrix reproduces it by exact integration. The lumped kinetic energy is \(hv^2/2\), because it assigns both end masses the full endpoint speed.

How would you isolate spatial dispersion from time stepping in a measurement of a cavity eigenfrequency?

Worked solution

Solve the generalised eigenproblem \(Kq=\omega^2Mq\) directly for increasingly fine meshes or converge time steps on each fixed mesh. Then compare with the continuum frequency and report both \(h\) and the time tolerance.

Chapter 17 · Moving a cell average without inventing or losing material

Derive a conservative transport step from cell overlap and measure how a monotone scheme spreads a pulse despite conserving its total mass exactly.

Transport is a translation

For constant \(a>0\), the solution to \(u_t+au_x=0\) is \(u(x,t)=u_0(x-at)\). Consider periodic cells of width \(h\). Over one step \(\Delta t\), a cell translated back through distance \(a\Delta t\) overlaps part of its original cell and part of its left neighbour. If the initial reconstruction is constant in each cell and \(C=a\Delta t/h\) lies between zero and one, the new average is

\[u_j^{n+1}=(1-C)u_j^n+C u_{j-1}^n.\]

Summing all cells telescopes on a periodic grid, preserving total material \(h\sum_j u_j\). Each new value is a convex combination, so the maximum cannot increase and the minimum cannot fall. For \(C>1\) this particular two-cell overlap ceases to describe the translated interval.

Derive the spreading instead of reading it off a plot

Start from one unit of material in one cell and follow its distribution on an effectively unbounded grid. After one step its index increment behaves like a Bernoulli choice: zero with weight \(1-C\), one with weight \(C\). Repeated convolution gives a binomial profile. Its mean index after \(n\) steps is \(nC\) and variance \(nC(1-C)\). Multiplying by \(h^2\) and using \(n=t/\Delta t\) gives a physical variance \(a h(1-C)t\). Equating this with \(2D_\mathrm{num}t\) gives

\[D_\mathrm{num}=ah(1-C)/2.\]

This growth describes numerical spreading of a translated shape even though the scheme conserves mass.

Two ways to reach the same physical time

A pulse should move 12 cells. Taking 12 steps with \(C=1\) shifts data by exactly 12 indices. Taking 24 steps with \(C=0.5\) has the same mean displacement but an added variance of six cell widths squared. In a source-free transport test, peak height and width reveal an error hidden from the total-mass budget.

Open this experiment online

The experiment starts with one full cell on a ring of 80 cells. Up to 40 steps avoid wraparound across the displayed pulse for the chosen starting index. The blackboard binomial calculation predicts centroid and spread independently of the plotted recurrence.

Tutorial on interpreting artificial diffusion

If \(C=0.25\) and the pulse has moved ten cells on average, what is its added variance?

Worked solution

Mean travel requires \(n=40\) steps. Variance in cell units is \(40(0.25)(0.75)=7.5\). Multiplying by \(h^2\) gives the physical variance.

Is a larger time step always the cure for pulse spreading?

Worked solution

For constant transport on this uniform grid \(C=1\) happens to align the translation with cell boundaries. Multiple speeds, nonuniform meshes and source physics generally preclude such alignment everywhere. A higher-order bounded reconstruction can reduce spreading while preserving conservation.

Read the university treatment

Use the advection examples to compare convergence in a norm with conservation of an integral and propagation of an extremum. Randall LeVeque · Finite Difference Methods for Ordinary and Partial Differential Equations

Chapter 18 · When diffusion destabilises a uniform state

Two scalar signals on a ring respond locally to each other and exchange signal with their neighbours. A single well-mixed cell returns to its reference state after a small disturbance. Can coupled cells amplify a spatial pattern? Derive a condition for this surprising outcome and test how faithfully a finite grid represents its wavelength.

Begin with a stable, spatially uniform experiment

Let \(u\) and \(v\) be small dimensionless departures from a reference operating point. They represent signed deviations, so their components can be negative without implying a negative absolute material quantity. Choose local time evolution \(u_t=u+v,\ v_t=-3u-2v\). A positive \(u\) tends to increase itself immediately; the second channel subsequently feeds back on the first. To determine whether a perturbation persists one must examine both channels together. Looking only at the positive diagonal coefficient in the first equation would give a wrong stability conclusion.

Write the state as a column and call the coefficient matrix \(J\). The characteristic polynomial of a two-by-two matrix is \(\mu^2-\mathrm{tr}(J)\mu+\det J\). Here the trace is minus one and the determinant is one. The two roots are \((-1\pm i\sqrt3)/2\). An isolated cell therefore has damped oscillatory motion for every initial perturbation; no normal mode has a positive growth rate. Some observables can still grow transiently in mixtures of modes. The present experiment asks about exponential growth of a mode over time.

Allow spatial exchange by adding \(D_u u_{xx}\) and \(D_v v_{xx}\) on a periodic ring of dimensionless circumference \(L\). Both diffusion coefficients are positive. Without coupling each field's Fourier component would decay at a rate proportional to the square of its wavenumber. The system changes qualitatively when the two diffusion rates differ because the path that restores local stability involves both fields. A spatially varying signal in one channel can be smoothed before it delivers the same feedback as in a spatially uniform experiment.

Reduce the field equation to one matrix per wavelength

Periodicity imposes \(k_m=2\pi m/L\) for integer \(m\). Write a normal mode as \(w\exp(ik_m x+\mu t)\). Differentiating the spatial factor twice gives \(-k_m^2\). Define \(q=k_m^2\). Substitution shows that \(\mu\) must be an eigenvalue of \(J-qD\), where \(D\) is diagonal with entries \(D_u,D_v\). Distinct modes do not interact in this spatially homogeneous linear model. One may analyse each mode separately and superpose the results.

\[\tau(q)=-1-(D_u+D_v)q,\]
\[\delta(q)=1-(D_v-2D_u)q+D_uD_v q^2.\]

The trace \(\tau\) is negative for every \(q\ge0\). Complex conjugate roots therefore have negative real parts. A positive eigenvalue must be real and accompanied by a negative one, so the determinant must be negative. Conversely, negative determinant ensures real eigenvalues of opposite signs. The instability question has become a test of the sign of a quadratic function on nonnegative arguments. Both at zero and at arbitrarily large \(q\) the determinant is positive. If a growing mode exists, it must occupy an intermediate range.

Put \(b=D_v-2D_u\) and \(c=D_uD_v\). The minimum occurs at \(q=b/(2c)\), which is positive only if \(b>0\). Its value is \(1-b^2/(4c)\). Thus the continuous dispersion curve contains an open interval of growing wavenumbers if \(b>0\) and \(b^2>4c\). At equality one neutral wavenumber appears; a neutral mode neither grows nor establishes instability. The physically allowed mode numbers of the finite ring must still place some squared wavenumber inside the interval. A condition derived for a continuum of wavelengths alone can overpredict what fits in a short apparatus.

\[q_\pm=\frac{b\pm\sqrt{b^2-4c}}{2c},\qquad q_-<k_m^2<q_+.\]

Calculate the unstable band before running a simulation

Choose \(D_u=0.02,D_v=1\). Then

\[b=0.96,\ c=0.02.\]

The two roots of the determinant are about 1.065 and 46.93. On a ring of circumference \(2\pi\), \(k_m=m\), so the continuum equation amplifies modes two through six. The uniform mode still decays. Mode one has \(q=1\), just below the lower edge, while mode seven has \(q=49\), just above the upper edge. Both are valuable tests because a moderate numerical error in the dispersion relation could reverse their classification.

The largest real part of the two eigenvalues is \(\operatorname{Re}[\tau+\sqrt{\tau^2-4\delta}]/2\). Choose the square root with nonnegative real part. Where the discriminant is negative the real part of the square root is zero; treating a negative discriminant as an error and dropping those wavenumbers would omit damped oscillatory physics. At \(q=4\), the determinant is \(-2.52\) and the trace is \(-5.08\). The positive rate is approximately 0.4553. During one dimensionless time unit an initially pure growing eigenmode at that wavenumber is multiplied by about 1.58.

Derive what a particular spatial difference sees

Now discretise the ring with \(N\) equally spaced points of spacing \(h=L/N\). The centred second difference of a discrete Fourier mode multiplies it by \((e^{ikh}-2+e^{-ikh})/h^2\). Using the cosine half-angle identity gives a real, nonpositive factor. Compare it with the exact factor \(-k^2\). They agree to leading order at small \(kh\) but separate substantially near the grid scale.

\[q_h(k)=\frac{4}{h^2}\sin^2(kh/2)=k^2-\frac{h^2k^4}{12}+O(h^4k^6).\]

For each sampled mode the finite grid uses \(J-q_hD\). The same algebraic determinant test applies, with a different argument. On the 32-point default grid, mode seven has \(q_h\) about 41.8 rather than 49. Its grid growth rate is positive even though its continuum rate is negative. This discrepancy survives any refinement of the time integrator on that fixed grid. The early growth has a reproducible numerical explanation and can be diagnosed without waiting for a long transient simulation.

A meaningful refinement experiment must follow a fixed physical mode as \(N\) increases. Following the highest mode of each grid instead changes its physical wavelength. Similarly, changing circumference while keeping the mode number fixed changes the wavelength and therefore the physical growth rate. The notebook exposes both controls so the two experiments can be distinguished. Large-scale modes can appear converged while the classification at the upper band edge remains sensitive to cell count.

Relate an eigenvalue to a picture of a pattern

Construct a numerical experiment in which the expected time dependence is actually known. For each of several low spatial modes, use the leading eigenvector of the two-channel finite-grid matrix and multiply by a small, controlled initial amplitude. Evolve its coefficient by its complex exponential, then take real parts to obtain real sensor signals. The resulting trace has no time-stepping error. An independently assembled full block matrix, including the periodic connection between the first and last cells, supplies another way to recover the largest growth rate. The optional OneSimulation eigensolver applies to this complete matrix directly.

A visible crest in a total field is not a single Fourier coefficient. It can move or change height through interference even when each included mode is decaying. To measure a rate compare the amplitude of one spatial Fourier component at two times. If the initial field contains both eigenvectors at that same wavenumber, the coefficient may not yet show one pure exponential. Preparing a known eigenvector or observing later, while the state remains within the linear range, makes the inference interpretable.

There is also an upper limit to what this calculation predicts. A linear mode with positive rate grows exponentially without saturation. Once its amplitude is comparable to the scale used to linearise the original response, nonlinear terms could shift the wavelength selection, mix modes or produce a bounded state. The notebook chooses small initial amplitudes and finite observation times precisely to isolate onset. It cannot infer the final pattern simply by extending the linear growth curve indefinitely.

Set a resolution requirement from the measured quantity

Near the upper band edge the question can be posed in terms of a tolerance on \(q\). A finite-grid prediction of a positive rate changes sign precisely when its effective \(q_h\) crosses the edge. If the target mode has \(q\) barely above that edge, estimate whether the leading truncation bias \(h^2q^2/12\) exceeds the distance to it. This supplies an inexpensive grid-design calculation before allocating a time integrator. Because the expansion assumes \(kh\) is small, verify the final decision with the exact sine symbol.

Away from an edge the sign can already be reliable while the predicted doubling time is inaccurate. A positive growth rate corresponds to a doubling time of \(\ln2/\mu\); relative errors are strongly amplified near neutral stability if measured against a very small rate. Record both the sign and an absolute uncertainty on the rate when comparing computations near onset. A single label such as stable or unstable does not communicate that sensitivity.

Tutorial on modes and finite domains

1. Give a short proof that equal positive diffusion coefficients cannot create an instability in this model.

Work through the solution

If \(D_u=D_v=d\), the spatial block is \(J-dqI\). Its eigenvectors remain those of the stable local system, and every eigenvalue shifts left by \(dq\). All real parts are at most minus one half. This argument works for any locally stable constant matrix with identical scalar diffusion in every channel.

2. Keep the two default diffusion coefficients but change the ring circumference to \(\pi\). Which positive continuum mode numbers grow?

Work through the solution

The allowed wavenumbers are \(k_m=2m\). The inequality becomes \(1.065<4m^2<46.93\). It admits \(m=1,2,3\), corresponding to wavenumbers 2, 4 and 6. Comparing mode numbers alone between the two rings would hide this wavelength correspondence.

3. A Fourier amplitude rises from \(2\cdot10^{-4}\) to \(8\cdot10^{-4}\) in four time units. Estimate the growth rate and describe an additional measurement needed before identifying a physical instability.

Work through the solution

A pure mode gives \(\mu=\log(4)/4\simeq0.3466\) per time unit. Confirm that the same physical wavenumber grows on refined grids and that its rate approaches the continuum dispersion calculation. A positive finite-grid rate by itself cannot distinguish a real band from the extra mode admitted by a coarse Laplacian.

Problem sheet 7 · Project · Verify a heat solver before interpreting its temperature

A laboratory plate can be heated internally and cooled at its boundary. Check global feasibility, the spatial error between nodes and the wavelength-dependent decay of periodic modes.

Can the thermal problem have a steady solution?

A one-dimensional bar of unit length and cross section obeys \(-u''=2\) in scaled units. Prescribe outward conductive fluxes \(q_L=0.5\), \(q_R=1.2\). Integrate the equation before solving. What physical quantity changes with time under these fluxes? Correct the right flux while keeping the left.

Work through the solution

Outward flux is \(-u'n\). Total outward flux must equal the integrated source, 2. The prescribed sum is 1.7, leaving a positive heating imbalance of 0.3. With finite heat capacity the mean temperature rises. A compatible right flux is 1.5. A steady calculation with purely Neumann data then needs a reference temperature. Use the Neumann compatibility experiment. Its left sensor reports zero rightward flux. For this comparison set \(v=u-0.5x\), which shifts the rightward flux by +0.5 at both ends. The original measurement then corresponds to the slider setting “Right flux minus 2” of −0.30, and the corrected data correspond to zero.

Fix the reference and check signs

For the compatible data, find the quadratic solution with zero spatial mean. Verify both fluxes and show which part a gradient measurement cannot recover.

Work through the solution

Integrating twice gives \(u=-x^2+0.5x+C\) because at the left \(q_L=u'(0)=0.5\). At the right \(-u'(1)=1.5\). The mean is \(-1/3+1/4+C\), so \(C=1/12\). Adding any constant leaves every flux and gradient unchanged. A measured mean supplies information absent from the differential equation.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Explain the missing inter-element curvature

Interpolate that exact quadratic linearly on uniform elements of width \(h\). Derive the peak pointwise interpolation error on one element and the jump in its piecewise-constant derivative between adjacent elements. Predict what happens when \(h\) halves. Compare the two errors with the residual and refinement example, which uses unequal sources on the two halves and reports a global energy norm.

Work through the solution

On \([a,b]\), \(u-I_hu=-(x-a)(x-b)\), which peaks at \(h^2/4\). The slope of each secant equals the exact derivative at its midpoint. Moving to the next element changes this slope by \(-2h\). Halving \(h\) quarters the peak value error and halves the jump magnitude. The adaptive experiment measures the gradient error in an energy norm. Uniformly halving all element widths would halve that norm for this quadratic. Selective refinement can give a different global ratio. Machine-small residual in the assembled linear system alone measures neither the field error nor the derivative jump.

Test an independent transient boundary condition

Now join the ends periodically. For \(u_t=u_{xx}\) on a unit ring, a sine of mode \(m\) decays at rate \((2\pi m)^2\). Derive the semi-discrete finite-difference rate and evaluate its ratio to the true rate at eight points per wavelength.

Work through the solution

A Fourier mode on the grid acquires factor \(-4\sin^2(\pi m h)/h^2\) under the centred second difference. At eight points per wavelength, the ratio is \([\sin(\pi/8)/(\pi/8)]^2\approx0.94964\). Numerical decay is about 5% too slow even with exact time integration. Compare with the modal diffusion experiment with 16 nodes and mode 2. The frequency and domain scaling differ, but the decay-rate ratio depends only on points per wavelength. Periodic and flux tests isolate distinct boundary and discretisation errors.

Problem sheet 8 · Resolve an outflow layer by fitting the numerical face flux

A dilute solute moves down a channel and diffuses against its mean flow. Fixed concentrations at the ends generate a thin downstream layer. Construct the cell flux directly from the differential equation, then ask how a solver can return nearly the right total throughput while still predicting an impossible local concentration.

Solve the physical flux problem before discretising

Let \(x\in[0,1]\), positive constant speed \(a\), and constant diffusivity \(D\). Steady conservation gives \(\partial_x J=0\) with \(J=au-Du_x\). Impose \(u(0)=1\), \(u(1)=0\). Find \(u(x)\) and the flux. Where must the sharp gradient occur when \(a/D\) is large?

Work through the solution

Constant flux turns the equation into \(u'-(a/D)u=-J/D\). Multiplication by \(e^{-ax/D}\) and integration gives a constant plus an exponential. The boundary values yield

\[u(x)=[1-e^{-(a/D)(1-x)}]/[1-e^{-a/D}],\]
\[J=a/[1-e^{-a/D}].\]

Far upstream for large \(a/D\) the concentration stays near one and most of the flux is advective. At the downstream wall the concentration is zero, but the diffusive term carries the same positive flux. The layer thickness scale is \(D/a\), a length. Constant throughput requires convection and diffusion to trade roles across the layer.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Derive the Scharfetter–Gummel face relation

Take a segment from \(x_i\) to \(x_{i+1}\) of length \(h\) with given endpoint concentrations \(u_i,u_{i+1}\). Solve the same constant-coefficient equation across that segment and express the face flux in a form with a well-defined zero-velocity limit.

Work through the solution

Define \(P=ah/D\) and \(B(P)=P/(e^P-1)\) with \(B(0)=1\). Integrating between the endpoints gives

\[u_{i+1}=e^P u_i-(J/a)(e^P-1).\]

Solving for the common flux and using \(B(-P)=e^P B(P)\) yields

\[J_{i+1/2}=(D/h)[B(-P)u_i-B(P)u_{i+1}].\]

At \(P=0\) it reduces to the centred diffusive flux. For large positive \(P\), \(B(P)\) becomes small while \(B(-P)\) approaches \(P\); the advective part then uses the upstream value. Both coefficients stay positive for real \(P\), supporting monotone solutions of this one-dimensional problem.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Numerical evaluation requires more than inserting the formula literally. Near zero use \(P/\operatorname{expm1}(P)\) or the series \(1-P/2+P^2/12+\cdots\). At very large positive \(P\), use \(B(P)=P e^{-P}/(1-e^{-P})\) to avoid exponential overflow. The identity \(B(-P)=B(P)+P\) avoids a second evaluation. Equal face fluxes across adjacent cells impose local conservation: whatever leaves one control volume enters its neighbour. For constant properties and no internal source, the exact local solution on each segment matches the analytic global nodal values, even when few grid points lie within the layer.

Diagnose a nearly correct throughput with impossible concentrations

Set \(a=1\), \(D=1/24\) and use eight equal segments. Compare the penultimate nodal concentration from exponential fitting, centred advection and first-order upwinding. Why can global throughput hide the error?

Work through the solution

The cell Péclet number is three. For any two-point constant flux relation with constant coefficients the homogeneous nodal variation is \(r^i\). Exponential fitting gives \(r=e^P\); centred advection gives

\[r=(1+P/2)/(1-P/2)=-5;\]

upwinding gives

\[r=1+P=4.\]

With these Dirichlet values the solution is \((r^N-r^i)/(r^N-1)\). At node seven the three values are approximately \(0.9502,\ 1.2000,\ 0.7500\). The centred approximation exceeds the inflow concentration and alternates around it because \(r\) is negative. Upwinding preserves the admissible interval but makes the layer broader. Exponential fitting gives the true nodal value for this local model.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

For each scheme, a constant concentration transports advective flux \(au\), while its \(r^i\) homogeneous part has zero net flux. The numerical throughput consequently equals \(a r^N/(r^N-1)\). With eight cells the centred scheme predicts about \(1.00000256\), remarkably close to the true \(1.00000000004\), despite its local overshoot of twenty percent. A scale measuring total output could validate the wrong spatial solver. Report a pointwise quantity, a monotonicity check and flux conservation in addition to throughput if the concentration near the outlet matters.

Test fitted nodes and resolve the inside of a cell

Vary the number of segments and the global Péclet number in the live graph. Under which interpretation is exponential fitting exact, and how could a plotted straight line through its last two values mislead an experimenter?

Work through the solution

Under the specified homogeneous coefficients, zero sources and exact boundary data, the fitted scheme reproduces the analytic solution at every node and returns its correct constant flux. It does not make a piecewise-linear drawing between those nodes the exact local profile. With eight cells at global Péclet number 24, the midpoint of the last cell is \(1/16\) from the outlet. The exact concentration is about \(1-e^{-1.5}=0.777\); linear interpolation between \(0.9502\) and zero gives only \(0.4751\). Reconstruction inside the cell requires the exponential solution with the two endpoint values, or more resolution. Distinguish the face flux actually used in the method from a derivative estimated by visually differentiating the drawn polyline.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Try cell Péclet numbers just below and above two. The centred solution changes from a very sharp monotone profile to a sign-alternating boundary correction. The fitted flux changes continuously through the same transition. If coefficients vary significantly within a cell or a source deposits mass inside it, one needs a corresponding local model, source integration or refinement. An exact result for one constant-coefficient test helps isolate the subsequent approximation. It cannot establish accuracy for every heterogeneous transport problem.
