Spatial discretisation and field equations

Derive a discrete field problem from a continuous equation, choose appropriate boundary data and test the convergence of quantities a measurement could resolve.

  1. Choose the physically admissible wave in a conservative update

    A discontinuous scalar flow can satisfy integral conservation in more than one way. Derive the shock speed, use a convex entropy to distinguish a compression from an expansion and build a finite-volume flux from the resulting local solution.

  2. Bound diffusion error by balancing a reconstructed flux

    A temperature field from a finite-element calculation looks smooth. Reconstruct a heat flux which exactly balances the source, then derive an upper bound for the true energy error without knowing the exact temperature.

  3. A Fourier Poisson solver and the trouble caused by unresolved products

    In a periodic medium the constant part of a source decides whether a Poisson problem has any solution. Derive the Fourier solver, separate spectral and finite-difference symbols, and see how a nonlinear product can inject a false low-frequency source.

  4. How much of an outgoing wave reflects from the numerical boundary?

    A finite grid truncates an effectively open medium. Derive an exact one-dimensional radiation condition, predict the angular reflection of its two-dimensional extension and design a pulse experiment that separates boundary reflection from interior dispersion.

  5. Can a stable finite-element heat step turn a cold node negative?

    Start a conducting rod with one positive temperature pulse. Assemble the heat capacity from first principles and determine when an explicit step respects both decay and the sign of every temperature.

  6. Solving two-dimensional diffusion one direction at a time

    Derive a second-order alternating-direction step, identify the boundary data required halfway through it and distinguish splitting error from mesh and time errors.

  7. Following the error from one reference triangle to an entire mesh

    Derive a first-order energy error estimate and a second-order displacement estimate for a conforming linear triangular discretisation. Track the assumption on element shape at the point where it enters the proof.

  8. Which waves are allowed to enter a numerical boundary?

    Diagonalise linear acoustics, impose one physical condition per incoming wave and compare diffusive and dispersive transport schemes on the same spatial Fourier mode.

Spatial discretisation and field equations · 1. Choose the physically admissible wave in a conservative update

A discontinuous scalar flow can satisfy integral conservation in more than one way. Derive the shock speed, use a convex entropy to distinguish a compression from an expansion and build a finite-volume flux from the resulting local solution.

Integrate across a moving discontinuity

Consider inviscid Burgers' equation \(u_t+(u^2/2)_x=0\). Where \(u\) is smooth its characteristics move at speed \(u\). For left state \(u_L>u_R\), faster left characteristics catch slower right ones and the profile steepens. If discontinuity position is \(x=st\), integrate the conservation law across a narrow moving control volume. The storage change from the moving boundary gives \(s(u_R-u_L)=f(u_R)-f(u_L)\), so for unequal states \(s=(u_L+u_R)/2\). The value is a conservation-law jump speed; applying the pointwise product \(u\,u_x\) across a discontinuity without defining it could predict another speed.

The same jump calculation formally allows \(u_L<u_R\), although a smooth monotone connection then spreads. To identify the physical weak solution, add a small positive viscosity \(\nu u_{xx}\) and consider its limiting dissipation. For convex entropy \(\eta=u^2/2\) with entropy flux \(q=u^3/3\), multiplication of the viscous equation by \(u\) gives

\[\eta_t+q_x=\nu\eta_{xx}-\nu u_x^2.\]

As viscosity vanishes the entropy production is nonpositive in distributions. Across a sharp jump the coefficient is

\[[q]-s[\eta]=(u_R-u_L)^3/12,\]

where brackets mean right minus left. It is negative for a compression shock and positive for an expansion shock. Thus the latter satisfies mass conservation but violates this entropy criterion.

Construct the rarefaction from its self-similar coordinate

For a left-to-right increase seek \(u(x,t)=U(\xi)\) with \(\xi=x/t\). Substituting into the smooth equation gives \((U-\xi)U'=0\), so each region is constant or \(U=\xi\). Matching both states yields \(u_L\) for \(\xi<u_L\), \(\xi\) for \(u_L\le\xi\le u_R\), and \(u_R\) beyond. This continuous fan is the physically selected expansion. With \(u_L=-1,u_R=1\), the origin lies inside the fan and \(u(0,t)=0\). A stationary expansion jump would misleadingly carry flux \(1/2\) across that interface rather than the physical zero.

In a finite-volume scheme cell averages are updated by shared interface fluxes:

\[\bar u_i^{n+1}=\bar u_i^n-(\Delta t/\Delta x)(F_{i+1/2}-F_{i-1/2}).\]

Solve the local Riemann problem between the piecewise-constant left and right cell states and take \(F=f(U(0))\). For a compression use the left state if \(s>0\) and the right if \(s<0\); either flux agrees for a stationary shock. For an expansion reaching across zero take zero; if the expansion moves wholly right use its left state, and if wholly left use its right. These cases give the scalar Godunov flux. The update telescopes internally because both neighbouring cells use exactly the same physical interface flux.

Distinguish shock location from smoothness and numerical thickness

A first-order Godunov discretisation under its CFL restriction is monotone and avoids spurious oscillations, but a moving discontinuity generally spreads over several cells. A centred pointwise difference may look sharper for smooth data while producing oscillations or the wrong propagation under discontinuous data. Test shock speeds from integrated cell mass or a consistent location reconstruction, compare fan areas and measure \(L^1\) error under refinement. The pointwise maximum error right at a jump can remain large even as the location and integral error converge. A visually thin front is not by itself an accuracy certificate.

For data \(u_L=1,u_R=-0.5\) the shock moves right at 0.25. At \(t=0.25\) it lies at \(x=0.0625\). In the interval \([-1,1]\) with unchanged boundary states, the total mass begins at 0.5 and grows by \([f(1)-f(-0.5)]t=(0.5-0.125)0.25=0.09375\), so the exact integral is 0.59375. A conservative numerical calculation can achieve this integral essentially to roundoff while retaining a visibly smeared shock. Conservation, entropy selection and resolved spatial profile are three independent questions.

Switch between a shock and a transonic fan

The experiment solves the finite-volume update on \([-1,1]\) with constant exterior states, a time step chosen for maximum CFL 0.8 and output time 0.25. Compare the conservative cell averages with the exact self-similar profile. Reverse both initial states to turn a compression into an expansion, then put the fan across zero. Increasing cell count changes numerical thickness, while the mass should follow the boundary flux difference at every resolution. Equal left and right states are a separate constant-preservation test.

Open this experiment online

Infer shock position from an integral measurement

A blurred profile does not require an arbitrary definition of its centre. Suppose the plateau values on \([-L,L]\) are measured reliably and a single discontinuity lies at \(X\). Its integrated state is \(M=(L+X)u_L+(L-X)u_R\). The equivalent sharp-interface position is therefore

\[X=[M-L(u_L+u_R)]/(u_L-u_R).\]

With \(L=1,\ u_L=1,\ u_R=-1/2\), a cell-sum reading \(M=0.59375\) gives \(X=0.0625\). A mass uncertainty of \(0.003\) gives a position uncertainty of \(0.002\) in the same coordinates. The inference becomes ill-conditioned as the plateau contrast tends to zero.

This provides a mesh-independent diagnostic when numerical viscosity smears the jump. Track both an area-based position and a measure of thickness, for example the distance between the two intermediate levels one-quarter and three-quarters across the jump. A conservative solver may retain a highly accurate area-based position while thickness changes strongly under refinement. In a rarefaction the same scalar area exists, but two real moving edges delimit a resolved fan. Replacing the fan by one jump would throw away precisely the information the entropy condition supplied.

Tutorial · compare conservation and entropy

Calculate one update next to a moving shock

With states 1 and \(-0.5\), take \(\Delta t/\Delta x=0.5\). Find the interfacial flux and the new values in the two cells adjacent to the discontinuity, assuming homogeneous neighbours farther out.

Work through the solution

The shock speed is positive, so Godunov flux at the jump is \(f(1)=0.5\). The adjacent left cell sees equal incoming and outgoing fluxes and stays 1. The adjacent right cell sees incoming 0.5 and outgoing 0.125, so becomes

\[-0.5+0.5(0.375)=-0.3125.\]

This is exactly the average associated with movement of the shock through one eighth of that right cell: \(0.125(1)+0.875(-0.5)=-0.3125\).

Check a conservative expansion jump

For \(-1\to1\), show that a stationary discontinuity satisfies mass conservation but give its entropy violation. Which profile should occupy \(x=0.10\) at \(t=0.25\)?

Work through the solution

Both end fluxes equal 0.5, so the formal discontinuity speed is zero. Its entropy coefficient is \((1-(-1))^3/12=2/3>0\), contradicting dissipation. The rarefaction has \(\xi=x/t=0.4\) inside its fan, hence \(u=0.4\) there. A scheme retaining a stationary sharp expansion jump can pass a mass check and still fail this physical verification.

Set a stable step and predict a conserved integral

On 100 uniform cells in \([-1,1]\) take \(u_L=-0.25,u_R=0.75\). What step gives maximum CFL 0.75? What mass is expected at time 0.20?

Work through the solution

Here \(\Delta x=0.02\) and maximum speed is 0.75, giving \(\Delta t=0.020\). Initial mass is \(0.5\). Boundary flux difference is \(0.03125-0.28125=-0.25\); over time 0.20 the mass falls to 0.45. Both fan edges are still far from the domain ends. For longer runs boundary data and outgoing-wave treatment would alter this budget.

Spatial discretisation and field equations · 2. Bound diffusion error by balancing a reconstructed flux

A temperature field from a finite-element calculation looks smooth. Reconstruct a heat flux which exactly balances the source, then derive an upper bound for the true energy error without knowing the exact temperature.

Identify the natural error measure in a diffusion problem

Consider \(-\nabla\cdot(k\nabla u)=f\) on a bounded domain, with homogeneous Dirichlet temperature for clarity and strictly positive scalar conductivity \(k(\mathbf x)\) bounded above and below. The weak form is \(\int k\nabla u\cdot\nabla w=\int f w\) for every test \(w\) vanishing on the boundary. A conforming approximate temperature \(v\) satisfies the prescribed boundary trace but may not solve the equation. Its energy error \(\|\nabla(u-v)\|_k^2=\int k|\nabla(u-v)|^2\) weights local gradient errors according to their physical conductivity. This differs from pointwise temperature error, total dissipation error or the absolute error of one designated sensor.

Define true heat flux \(q=-k\nabla u\). It obeys \(\nabla\cdot q=f\). Suppose a reconstructed flux \(\sigma\) obeys the same divergence exactly and has the prescribed normal flux on any Neumann boundary, with normal component continuous across interior mesh faces. Such fluxes belong naturally to a space often written \(H(\mathrm{div})\). Conforming temperature and equilibrated flux are separate requirements: a continuous piecewise-linear temperature gives generally discontinuous normal gradient flux across faces, even if all global nodal equations were solved accurately.

Prove an upper bound by cancelling a cross term

Take the weighted constitutive defect \(\sigma+k\nabla v=(\sigma-q)+k\nabla(v-u)\). Expand its squared \(k^{-1}\) norm. The cross term is \(2\int(\sigma-q)\cdot\nabla(v-u)\). Integrating by parts gives a volume term proportional to \(\nabla\cdot(\sigma-q)=0\) and a boundary term zero where \(v-u=0\) or the normal flux difference is prescribed zero. Interior normal continuity prevents an extra jump term. Hence

\[\|\sigma+k\nabla v\|_{k^{-1}}^2=\|\sigma-q\|_{k^{-1}}^2+\|\nabla(v-u)\|_k^2 .\]

The left side can be calculated from the approximation and reconstruction. It bounds the unknown energy error from above, with equality precisely when the reconstructed flux equals the true flux. A divergence-compatible but inaccurate \(\sigma\) still certifies an upper bound, albeit possibly a loose one. This identity is an instance of the Prager–Synge hypercircle principle. With approximated data or inexact equilibration, extra data oscillation and residual terms must be bounded before calling an indicator a guaranteed bound.

Background · How does a nonzero flux imbalance enter the certificate?

Take heat flux \(q=-k\nabla u\), so that \(\nabla\cdot q=f\). Suppose a reconstructed flux \(\hat q\) leaves a source residual \(r=f-\nabla\cdot\hat q\), and the approximate and exact temperatures agree on the entire Dirichlet boundary. Testing the residual equation with their difference \(e=u-u_h\) gives

\[\int k|\nabla e|^2=\int r e-\int(\hat q+k\nabla u_h)\cdot\nabla e.\]

With \(k\geq k_{\min}>0\) and a Poincaré constant \(C_P\) such that \(\|e\|\le C_P\|\nabla e\|\), the energy norm is bounded by \(\|k^{-1/2}(\hat q+k\nabla u_h)\|+C_P\|r\|/\sqrt{k_{\min}}\). The second term is the price of imbalance. If source data have been projected onto simpler functions during reconstruction, include their residual against the actual source before calling the result a bound.

Carry out the entire construction on two elements

On \([0,1]\) let \(k=1,\ f=2,\ u(0)=u(1)=0\). The exact \(u=x(1-x)\) is available for checking although unnecessary for the estimator. The conforming piecewise-linear interpolant on two half-elements has slopes \(+0.5,-0.5\). Any flux \(\sigma(x)=2x+C\) exactly balances the source. Choose \(C\) to minimise \(\int(\sigma+v')^2dx\). Its derivative is \(2\int(2x+C+v')dx=2(1+C)\), since \(v(1)-v(0)=0\). Thus \(C=-1\), giving \(\sigma=2x-1=q\). The estimator is \(\sqrt{1/12}=0.288675\): on each half interval the defect is a linear ramp from \(-0.5\) to \(+0.5\) or its translated equivalent. Direct integration of \((u'-v')^2\) confirms equality.

If instead choose \(C=-0.8\), source equilibrium is still exact but the flux carries a uniform 0.2 offset. Its squared error contribution is 0.04, giving bound \(\sqrt{1/12+0.04}=0.3512\). Choosing an equilibrated flux means satisfying local balance; optimising its closeness to the constitutive flux sharpens the estimate. The true temperature error is unchanged in both comparisons. In more dimensions mixed finite elements or local face-flux reconstructions enforce shared normal flux degrees of freedom; local least-squares choices can improve sharpness without requiring a global exact solution.

Use element contributions without confusing local and global bounds

The squared estimator splits as \(\sum_K\int_K k^{-1}|\sigma+k\nabla v|^2\). Large element contributions can guide refinement and give a reproducible stopping rule for global energy error. The exact orthogonality proof, however, integrated over the full domain. Boundary terms between elements may fail to vanish on one element individually, so one local contribution need not be a strict independent upper bound on that element's actual error. For a particular output of interest, e.g. a mean temperature in a small sensor region, use the dual problem or a proven continuous inequality to translate energy uncertainty into output uncertainty.

Vary an equilibrated flux while holding the solution fixed

The source and exact quadratic temperature are those of the worked one-dimensional example. The plot compares the exact flux, the raw elementwise flux from the continuous linear temperature and a reconstructed flux with the correct divergence and a freely chosen constant offset. Changing this constant is permitted because the original problem specifies boundary temperatures. The mean energy-error contribution from an offset appears in quadrature with the true temperature-gradient error, exactly as in the derived identity. For two elements and offset 0.20 the bound is about 0.3512 while the actual energy error is 0.2887.

Increase the number of cells with the offset held fixed. The raw staircase becomes more accurate, but the reconstruction bound eventually plateaus near the absolute offset because the reconstructed flux still contains its deliberate uniform error. Set the offset to zero to recover the sharp certificate on each mesh. Notice that a constant flux offset preserves the source balance in this Dirichlet test; it would generally violate a specified Neumann flux at a boundary. A valid estimator depends as much on the boundary conditions as on the differential equation.

Tutorial · verify an error certificate rather than a residual plot

Predict refinement on a uniform mesh

Use the same quadratic exact solution and \(N\) equal elements. Find the energy error of the nodal interpolant and verify the two-element value.

Work through the solution

On each interval \(v'\) is the average of the linear exact derivative \(1-2x\). The derivative difference is a line of slope \(-2\) centred at the midpoint. Its squared integral is

\[4h^3/12=h^3/3.\]

Summing \(N\) terms gives error \(h/\sqrt3\). At \(h=1/2\) this equals \(1/\sqrt{12}\). Halving the element size halves the energy error even though the nodal temperature error for this interpolation is exactly zero at the nodes.

Diagnose a spuriously perfect estimator

An engineer chooses \(\sigma=-k\nabla v\) directly and gets a zero constitutive defect. Why does this not prove \(v=u\) for the two-element example?

Work through the solution

This piecewise-constant flux has zero derivative inside each element and a jump at the middle node. It fails the required divergence equation \(f=2\) as a distributed source and fails normal continuity. The missing volume and interface cross terms invalidate the orthogonality argument. A zero defect certifies a true solution only alongside both primal admissibility and flux equilibrium with the stated boundary conditions.

Bound the error of a mean temperature

On the unit interval with zero endpoint error, use \(\|w\|_2\le\|w'\|_2/\pi\) to bound the domain-mean error from the sharp two-element certificate. Compare with direct integration.

Work through the solution

Cauchy–Schwarz on a unit-length domain gives

\[|\int(u-v)|\le\|u-v\|_2\le0.288675/\pi=0.0919.\]

The exact integral of \(u\) is \(1/6\); the triangular interpolant has area \(1/8\), giving actual mean error \(1/24=0.04167\), within the bound. An error certificate for the gradient can yield a conservative output bound through an independently justified inequality; its units and domain constants cannot simply be omitted.

Spatial discretisation and field equations · 3. A Fourier Poisson solver and the trouble caused by unresolved products

In a periodic medium the constant part of a source decides whether a Poisson problem has any solution. Derive the Fourier solver, separate spectral and finite-difference symbols, and see how a nonlinear product can inject a false low-frequency source.

Find compatibility and gauge before dividing by a wave number

On a periodic interval of length \(2\pi\), solve \(-u''=f\). Integrating over one period gives \(0=\int f\) because periodic derivative values agree at the ends. This is an existence condition. If a solution exists, adding a constant changes neither field gradient nor source, so specify a mean \(u_0\) separately to fix uniqueness. These two roles sometimes get conflated in numerical code that simply sets the Fourier coefficient at zero to zero. Setting \(u_0=0\) fixes a gauge; deleting nonzero \(f_0\) removes a physical mean source and changes an incompatible model. Check the measured source mean before applying a projected solve.

For \(f(x)=\sum_k f_ke^{ikx}\), take \(u_k=f_k/k^2\) for \(k\ne0\). For a length \(L\) in physical units replace \(k\) by \(2\pi j/L\). A compatible source \(f=\cos3x+0.2\sin x\) gives mean-zero \(u=\cos3x/9+0.2\sin x\). Higher spatial frequencies are smoothed by the inverse Laplacian; data noise at low but nonzero wavenumber can affect the inferred field more strongly than visually rapid fluctuations of equal source amplitude. A compatible constant reference level of potential cannot be inferred from this equation even with noiseless source data.

Compare an exact spectral solve with a discrete operator

On \(N\) equispaced nodes, an FFT diagonalises any periodic translation-invariant discrete convolution, but the diagonal symbol depends on the operator being solved. A spectral second derivative uses \(k^2\) for \(-u''\), while a second-order periodic difference stencil uses \(\lambda_h(k)=4\sin^2(kh/2)/h^2\), \(h=2\pi/N\). The same FFT can invert either symbol. Verify a solver by applying the matching discrete operator back to its result. A Fourier result with tiny residual for the finite-difference stencil is a precise solution of that stencil; it does not thereby reproduce the continuum Fourier amplitude exactly.

At \(N=16,k=4\), \(kh/2=\pi/4\), hence \(\lambda_h=2/h^2=12.97\), compared with continuum \(k^2=16\). A unit cosine source has spectral potential amplitude 0.0625; the finite-difference inverse gives approximately 0.0771. Both can pass a discrete residual at machine precision in their appropriate systems. At fixed physical mode the symbols approach one another as \(N\) increases; at mode number constantly near the Nyquist limit their relative discrepancy need not vanish. A convergence test keeping \(k/N\) fixed follows different physical sources at each refinement.

Identify aliased modes before applying the inverse

For \(N\) samples, Fourier modes with indices differing by an integer multiple of \(N\) coincide at all nodes. Suppose a source includes \(v^2\), where \(v=\cos(5x)\) on a 12-point grid. Squaring exactly yields \([1+\cos(10x)]/2\). But sampled \(\cos10x=\cos2x\), so the grid reports an apparent low \(k=2\) component. Subtracting the known mean gives a compatible source, but the resulting potential amplitude becomes \(0.5/2^2=0.125\) at the wrong spatial frequency rather than \(0.5/10^2=0.005\) at the true one. The inverse Laplacian magnifies the false low-mode contribution, transforming a sampling error into a large field error.

To obtain the product projected on a retained Fourier band, zero-pad the input Fourier coefficients on a sufficiently enlarged grid, transform to space, multiply there, transform back and discard unretained output modes. For modes \(|k|\le K\), a quadratic product extends to \(2K\). To ensure none of its high modes folds back into the retained range, choose multiplication grid size strictly greater than \(3K\), or handle endpoint/Nyquist modes explicitly. This is the usual three-halves padding rule relative to a base grid around \(2K\). Different FFT normalisations require scaling when padding and truncating. Simply padding already multiplied samples cannot recover frequency information that has already aliased.

For a deliberately filtered small problem one can instead drop the top third of the spectrum on a fixed grid before nonlinear multiplication, retaining only \(|k|<N/3\). This avoids erroneous feedback into the retained modes but also discards legitimate high-frequency information. Choosing a finer physical grid can be preferable when the physical quantity of interest depends on those modes. Dealiasing corrects a numerical representation of the chosen projected equations; it does not supply a physical closure for unresolved turbulence, interfaces or material fluctuations.

Follow one unresolved harmonic through the Poisson inverse

The simulation multiplies a cosine of mode m by itself, removes its exact mean and inverts the periodic Poisson operator. The continuum curve retains the true product mode 2m. The unpadded curve uses whichever harmonic the coarse point samples represent, while the third curve shows the correctly projected solution restricted to the original resolvable Fourier band. At 12 samples and m equal to 5, coarse multiplication creates a false mode two whose potential amplitude is 25 times the desired continuum amplitude. The resolved-band projection correctly eliminates that harmonic rather than inventing a low mode.

Increase N until the true product is representable. The three answers then agree, including the phase of the spatial variation. The control limits the actual input frequency to a representable non-Nyquist input and reports the mode used, so changing the grid cannot silently inject an impossible input. A projected curve equal to zero means none of this particular product survives the retained band. It does not mean the continuum product or the underlying fine-scale field vanishes.

Tutorial · verify mean source, discrete response and nonlinear products

Detect an impossible boundary-value problem

A periodic source has \(f(x)=0.03+\sin x\). A program reports \(u=\sin x\) and a machine-small residual after dropping the zero Fourier mode. Evaluate the actual residual and explain its interpretation.

Work through the solution

For the reported solution \(-u''=\sin x\), so the original pointwise residual \(-u''-f=-0.03\) everywhere and its integral is \(-0.06\pi\). There is no periodic solution to the original equation. The programme exactly solved the mean-subtracted problem. An external sink or a physically justified zero-mean correction could make such a model appropriate, but the removed source must be stated and checked.

Predict the grid error of a low physical mode

For \(k=1\), expand \(\lambda_h(k)\) and find the leading relative error in the finite-difference solution amplitude for a fixed unit cosine source.

Work through the solution

Using \(\sin^2 z=z^2-z^4/3+\cdots\) gives

\[\lambda_h=k^2-k^4h^2/12+O(h^4).\]

Inversion yields

\[1/\lambda_h=k^{-2}[1+k^2h^2/12+O(h^4)].\]

The discrete potential amplitude is too large to leading order. Refining at the same \(k\) by halving \(h\) should reduce the amplitude error by roughly four. This statement differs from finding the residual of the already solved stencil.

Choose a padding size from the desired output band

Input modes occupy \(|k|\le5\), and only output modes \(|k|\le5\) of their quadratic product will be kept. Compare multiplication grids with 14 and 16 points.

Work through the solution

With 14 points a possible product \(k=10\) aliases to \(-4\), contaminating the retained range. With 16 points all modes \(6,\ldots,10\) which wrap do so outside \([-5,5]\); for example 10 wraps to \(-6\). Thus 16 is sufficient for retained coefficients. It is not sufficient to represent and report the complete \(k=10\) output wave itself, which needs a larger bandwidth. Distinguish accuracy of a projected nonlinear evolution from recovery of every generated physical harmonic.

Spatial discretisation and field equations · 4. How much of an outgoing wave reflects from the numerical boundary?

A finite grid truncates an effectively open medium. Derive an exact one-dimensional radiation condition, predict the angular reflection of its two-dimensional extension and design a pulse experiment that separates boundary reflection from interior dispersion.

Factor the one-dimensional wave equation

For a constant wave speed \(c>0\),

\[u_{tt}-c^2u_{xx}=(\partial_t-c\partial_x)(\partial_t+c\partial_x)u=0.\]

A right-moving waveform \(F(x-ct)\) satisfies \(u_t+c u_x=0\) and a left-moving waveform satisfies \(u_t-c u_x=0\). At the right boundary impose \(u_t+c u_x=0\) to transmit a purely outgoing right-moving wave; at the left boundary reverse the normal sign. This exact radiation condition requires no knowledge of \(F\) for the constant-coefficient 1D medium. A Dirichlet end instead forces a reflected amplitude minus the incident one, while a Neumann end reflects with the same sign. Both have unit reflected energy for an ideal undamped wave.

A separate energetic argument tests the sign. The interior energy \(E=\tfrac12\int(u_t^2+c^2u_x^2)dx\) has derivative \(dE/dt=c^2[u_tu_x]_{\rm left}^{\rm right}\). At a transmitting right end \(u_x=-u_t/c\), giving outward energy loss \(-c u_t^2\). Imposing the opposite sign there would permit energy injection. A dissipative boundary need not absorb perfectly: it can still reflect some of the incoming wave. The energy identity tests passivity, while a pulse-amplitude experiment tests reflection separately. An initially localised pulse that has not yet reached any boundary is a useful independent check of the interior solver.

Predict reflection as the incidence direction changes

For a flat boundary normal to \(+x\) in two dimensions, the local condition \(u_t+c u_x=0\) is exact only for normal incidence. Consider incident wave \(e^{i(k_xx+k_yy-\omega t)}\) and reflected wave of amplitude \(R\) with \(k_x\) reversed. Use \(\omega=c\sqrt{k_x^2+k_y^2}\) and place the boundary at \(x=0\). Substitution into the condition gives \((-\omega+ck_x)+R(-\omega-ck_x)=0\), hence

\[R=\frac{ck_x-\omega}{ck_x+\omega}=\frac{\cos\theta-1}{\cos\theta+1},\]

where \(\theta\) is measured from the outward normal. The reflected normal-energy fraction is \(|R|^2\) because incident and reflected magnitudes and normal speed factors match. Normal incidence gives zero. At \(60^\circ\), \(R=-1/3\) and power reflection \(1/9\); at grazing incidence its magnitude approaches one. An unspecified claim of a “nonreflecting boundary” has no definite quantitative meaning without frequency, angle, medium and discretisation.

An ideal exterior transparent condition can be expressed after transforming in tangential direction and time: \(\partial_x\hat u=ik_x\hat u\) with \(k_x=\sqrt{(\omega/c)^2-k_y^2}\) on the outgoing or decaying branch. The square root shows why a single local first derivative cannot cover all tangential frequencies exactly. Expanding the square root near normal incidence motivates higher-order absorbing boundary conditions; other methods use a carefully matched absorbing layer. The present first-order formula provides a baseline against which such alternatives can be tested. An evanescent component with imaginary normal wavenumber requires the decaying branch; interpreting it as a propagating beam with a real incidence angle would be incorrect.

Measure boundary error independently of grid dispersion

An interior finite-difference solver usually has a numerical dispersion relation \(\omega_h(k_x,k_y)\). A discrete version of the continuum boundary condition need not be exactly matched even for a strongly resolved normally incident wave, particularly if boundary and interior stencils have different dispersion errors. To isolate the two effects, first propagate the same pulse on a much longer or periodic domain until it passes the observation probe and before any distant return. Record that reference. Then repeat with the open boundary placed closer and compare at the same probe over the predicted return window. Project signals onto spatial directions or separate incident and reflected time windows to estimate amplitude and energy.

For a probe distance \(D\) from a flat boundary and a narrow incident beam at angle \(\theta\), normal group speed is \(c\cos\theta\). The time between incident and reflected peaks measured on a normal coordinate is roughly \(2D/(c\cos\theta)\), provided the probe or array also samples the reflected beam's tangential position. A point probe on an oblique pencil beam need not see both pulses at all; an extended plane-wave excitation or a probe array is more suitable for estimating the stated reflection coefficient. Run a sequence of grid refinements at fixed physical angle and wavelength. Reflection approaching the analytical \(|R|\) indicates continuum boundary-model error; reflection shrinking toward zero at normal incidence indicates discretisation mismatch.

Measure a boundary reflection across incidence angles

The plotted energy fractions follow directly by matching an incoming and a reflected plane wave at a flat boundary. One curve uses the physically correct wave speed in the first-order condition; the other lets the specified boundary speed differ from the interior. Use the angle slider to inspect the signed amplitude as well as its squared magnitude. At matched speed and sixty degrees the amplitude is minus one third and the reflected energy is about 11.11 percent. At normal incidence the matched boundary absorbs the incident plane wave completely.

Choose a boundary speed larger than the interior speed. There is then an oblique angle at which the mismatch exactly compensates the projected normal phase in this local equation. It obeys cosine of the angle equal to the reciprocal speed ratio. For a ratio 1.25 this is about 36.87 degrees. Matching this one direction sacrifices perfect normal incidence; it is not an omnidirectional improvement. The graph concerns the continuous condition. An actual spatial discretisation can add reflection through numerical dispersion, which the longer-domain pulse comparison in this unit is designed to detect.

Tutorial · identify the return signal

Determine the admissible incidence angle for one-percent reflected energy

Solve \(|R|^2\le0.01\) for a real outgoing incidence angle \(0\le\theta<90^\circ\).

Work through the solution

The amplitude criterion is \((1-\cos\theta)/(1+\cos\theta)\le0.1\), so

\[\cos\theta\ge0.9/1.1=0.81818.\]

This gives \(\theta\lesssim35.1^\circ\). Requiring one-percent amplitude instead would impose a stricter angle. A specification should state whether it concerns a field amplitude or a quadratic energy measurement.

Calculate reflection from an incorrectly tuned 1D boundary

The physical medium has speed \(c\) but the implemented condition uses \(u_t+c_bu_x=0\) with \(c_b=1.20c\). Find the normally reflected amplitude and energy fraction.

Work through the solution

The right-travelling dispersion gives

\[k_x=\omega/c.\]

Repeating the boundary substitution yields

\[R=(c_b/c-1)/(c_b/c+1)=0.20/2.20=0.0909,\]

with energy reflection 0.00826. Its phase sign differs from the negative reflection arising for oblique incidence with a correctly tuned speed. A reflected waveform's sign can thus help diagnose a mismatch rather than reporting only its energy.

Design a time window to catch a weak return

A plane pulse propagates at 200 m/s and a line of probes is 3.0 m inside the boundary. Estimate normal-incidence and \(60^\circ\) return delays. Which comparisons are needed when a 2% amplitude is measured at normal incidence?

Work through the solution

The delays are

\[2(3)/200=0.030\,\mathrm{s},\]
\[2(3)/(200\times0.5)=0.060\,\mathrm{s}.\]

The continuum boundary predicts zero at normal incidence, so check interior numerical dispersion, boundary stencil, angular spread of the finite-width excitation, signal extraction and any physical material change near the boundary. A larger reference domain and refinement at fixed wavelength distinguish numerical return from pulse tails. A 2% amplitude corresponds to \(0.04\%\) reflected energy under matching impedances, not 2% energy.

Spatial discretisation and field equations · 5. Can a stable finite-element heat step turn a cold node negative?

Start a conducting rod with one positive temperature pulse. Assemble the heat capacity from first principles and determine when an explicit step respects both decay and the sign of every temperature.

Integrate the heat equation against a hat function

Take a uniform rod of length one with the two endpoint temperatures held at zero. With constant diffusivity \(\kappa>0\) the evolution is \(u_t=\kappa u_{xx}\). Approximate \(u(x,t)\) as a linear combination of continuous piecewise-linear hat functions. Multiply by an interior hat and integrate. Integration by parts transfers one spatial derivative onto each hat. The boundary term vanishes. The nodal temperature vector satisfies

\[M\dot u=-Ku.\]

On an element of length \(h\) the two hats are \(1-x/h\) and \(x/h\) in local coordinates. Integrating their products gives diagonal entries \(h/3\) and off-diagonal entries \(h/6\) in the element mass matrix. Their constant derivatives give diagonal stiffness \(\kappa/h\) and off-diagonal stiffness \(-\kappa/h\). After assembly and imposing Dirichlet values, the interior mass has diagonal \(4h/6\) and neighbouring entries \(h/6\). The stiffness has diagonal \(2\kappa/h\) and neighbours \(-\kappa/h\).

Forward Euler advances with \(G=I-\Delta t M^{-1}K\). On an eigenvector of \(K\phi=\lambda M\phi\) it multiplies the amplitude by \(1-\Delta t\lambda\). A bound \(\Delta t\lambda_{\max}\leq2\) therefore ensures no growth in the mass norm. Preserving nonnegative temperatures for every nonnegative initial vector requires every entry of \(G\) to be nonnegative. Each column is the response to a single positive nodal impulse, so the latter condition can be tested directly.

One alternative assigns half of each element's total heat capacity to each endpoint, before removing boundary degrees of freedom. Each interior mass then equals \(h\) and the explicit stencil has weights \(r,1-2r,r\), with \(r=\kappa\Delta t/h^2\). These coefficients are all nonnegative for \(0\leq r\leq 1/2\). The first and last interior equations also couple to the fixed zero boundary temperatures.

Compute the remote temperature after one update

With three interior nodes, \(h=1/4\), initially set the left interior node to 1 K above the boundary reference and both others to zero. Inverting the consistent mass in this three-by-three system gives the first column of \((h^2/\kappa)M^{-1}K\) as \((51,-36,9)/14\). At \(r=0.1\) the interior temperatures after one step become \((0.63571,0.25714,-0.06429)\) K. The cold node two edges away immediately drops below the lower reference.

The largest dimensionless eigenvalue is about 7.9223. The chosen step gives \(r\lambda_{\max}h^2/\kappa\approx0.7922\), comfortably inside the linear stability interval. Lumping instead gives \((0.8,0.1,0)\) K. The negative consistent-mass entry shrinks linearly when the step shrinks. For this impulse its sign persists for every positive first step. With OneSimulation installed locally, the notebook also constructs a physical line mesh and obtains the mass and stiffness by integrating its hat functions at mapped quadrature points. Compare the assembled result against the explicit tridiagonal entries. The experiment shows why reducing the time step can satisfy a stability test without restoring a discrete maximum principle.

Exercise · keep the lumped temperatures nonnegative

Suppose \(h=0.02\) m and \(\kappa=8\times10^{-5}\) m²/s. Find the greatest time step allowed by nonnegative weights. What stencil results at that step?

Work through the solution

The condition gives \(\Delta t=h^2/(2\kappa)=2.5\) s. The central coefficient vanishes and each adjacent value receives weight one half. At the first interior node the fixed boundary value supplies one of these two terms. A step slightly above this threshold gives a negative central coefficient and can drive an isolated positive impulse below zero.

Exercise · derive the largest mode on successively finer meshes

An interior sine wave has neighbouring sum \(2\cos(\theta)\) times its own nodal value. Use this fact to find the limiting stability condition for forward Euler with consistent mass as the number of uniform elements grows.

Work through the solution

The mass eigenvalue is \(h(2+\cos\theta)/3\) and the stiffness eigenvalue is \(2\kappa(1-\cos\theta)/h\). Their ratio, multiplied by \(h^2/\kappa\), is \(6(1-\cos\theta)/(2+\cos\theta)\). The largest allowed angle tends to \(\pi\), giving 12. The limiting stable step therefore has \(r\leq1/6\). A positivity test still inspects the entries of the update matrix separately.

Spatial discretisation and field equations · 6. Solving two-dimensional diffusion one direction at a time

Derive a second-order alternating-direction step, identify the boundary data required halfway through it and distinguish splitting error from mesh and time errors.

Factor the two-dimensional heat operator

For \(u_t=\kappa(u_{xx}+u_{yy})\) on a rectangle, first impose homogeneous Dirichlet data. Centred second differences on a Cartesian mesh give \(\dot U=(X+Y)U\), where \(X,Y\) are the directional diffusion matrices. For constant diffusivity and these boundaries the Kronecker factors commute and are symmetric negative definite. Put \(s=h/2\). Multiplying out a factored Crank–Nicolson step introduces \(s^2XY\) on both sides:

\[(I-sX)(I-sY)U^{n+1}=(I+sX)(I+sY)U^n .\]

Applied to a smooth solution the added defect is \(s^2XY(U^{n+1}-U^n)=O(h^3)\) at fixed mesh. The Peaceman–Rachford realisation uses line solves

\[(I-sX)U^*=(I+sY)U^n,\qquad (I-sY)U^{n+1}=(I+sX)U^*.\]

For a shared eigenmode with directional eigenvalues \(\lambda_x,\lambda_y\le0\), the amplification factor is the product of their midpoint factors. Each has modulus at most one and the product matches the exponential through order two. This argument establishes unconditional stability for the separable case. For highly damped modes a midpoint factor can change sign. Stability alone does not guarantee monotonicity or accurate damping.

Give the intermediate grid a mathematically consistent boundary

An intermediate variable solves an algebraic factor equation. It has no general obligation to equal the physical wall data at the midpoint time. For inhomogeneous boundaries choose a smooth lifting \(w\) whose trace equals the specified boundary values at all times, and evolve \(v=u-w\) with zero trace and forcing \(f+\kappa\Delta w-w_t\). For \(u=a(t)x\) with prescribed trace on all sides, \(w=a(t)x\) and \(f=a'(t)x\) give identically zero lifted field. A nonzero computed field in this manufactured case isolates a boundary or source inconsistency.

For a smooth lifted source \(g\), add \(s g(t_n+s)\) to both line-equation right sides. Expansion of the first stage and substitution into the second yields \(h g+h^2(X+Y)g/2\) through second order, along with the midpoint approximation to the source time variation. Mixing time levels at the wall can instead inject a first-order defect. Check that spatial derivatives of the lifted source stay resolved when taking a simultaneous time–space limit.

OneSimulation experiment · Propagate one heat mode in two directions

Double the spatial resolution while holding the final time fixed. Keep the grid-dependent reference in the comparison.

Download the executable notebook · Python source

Adjust the heat field and rotate its surface. Take initial data \(\sin(\pi x)\sin(q\pi y)\) on the unit square with zero wall values and \(\kappa=1\). Its directional grid eigenvalues are \(\lambda_x=-4\sin^2(\pi\Delta x/2)/\Delta x^2\) and \(\lambda_y=-4\sin^2(q\pi\Delta x/2)/\Delta x^2\). Each time step multiplies its amplitude by

\[G=\frac{1+h\lambda_x/2}{1-h\lambda_x/2}\frac{1+h\lambda_y/2}{1-h\lambda_y/2}.\]

The browser evaluates \(G^N\), exactly the mode reduction of the two matrix solves in the OneSimulation notebook. The rotatable surface shows the numerical field; its vertical coordinate is temperature, so this remains a two-dimensional spatial problem. The plot compares an antinode slice with both \(e^{(\lambda_x+\lambda_y)t}\) and the continuum amplitude \(e^{-\pi^2(1+q^2)t}\). First double the number of time steps on one fixed mesh, then refine the mesh. Choose a higher vertical mode and very few steps to see stable but inaccurate damping, sometimes with a reversed sign. A vertical reference from −1 to +1 keeps amplitude loss visible as the field cools.

Open the live experiment

"""Measure the temporal order of an ADI heat step against an exact grid mode."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import solve_dense

m = 9
spacing = 1/(m+1)
d = (np.diag(-2*np.ones(m)) + np.diag(np.ones(m-1), 1) + np.diag(np.ones(m-1), -1))/spacing**2
sine = np.sin(np.pi*spacing*np.arange(1, m+1))
initial = np.outer(sine, sine)
decay = -4*np.sin(np.pi*spacing/2)**2/spacing**2
end = 0.1
ref = initial*np.exp(2*decay*end)
steps, errors = np.array([2, 4, 8, 16, 32]), []
for count in steps:
    plus, minus = np.eye(m) + end*d/(2*count), np.eye(m) - end*d/(2*count)
    state = initial.copy()
    for _ in range(count):
        half = solve_dense(minus, state @ plus)
        state = solve_dense(minus, (plus @ half).T).T
    errors.append(np.max(abs(state-ref)))
orders = np.log2(np.array(errors[:-1])/errors[1:])
print(f"Finest-grid temporal order: {orders[-1]:.4f}")
assert abs(orders[-1]-2) < 0.01
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4), layout="constrained")
ax.loglog(end/steps, errors, "o-", label="ADI")
ax.loglog(end/steps, errors[-1]*(steps[-1]/steps)**2, "--", label="Quadratic reference")
ax.set(xlabel="Time step", ylabel="Maximum error relative to the spatially discrete mode")
ax.legend()
fig.savefig("numerics-adi-heat.png", dpi=170)

Output from the reference run

Finest-grid temporal order: 2.0003

Measure transport, time and splitting independently

A time-dependent transport–reaction problem can have noncommuting operators even on a periodic grid. Let \(Au=-c u_x,\ Bu=-k(x)u\). Direct differentiation gives \((AB-BA)u=c k'(x)u\). Expanding a product \(e^{hB}e^{hA}\) through \(h^2\) and subtracting \(e^{h(A+B)}\) leaves \(h^2(BA-AB)u/2\). Reversing the order reverses this leading defect. The symmetric composition \(e^{hA/2}e^{hB}e^{hA/2}\) cancels it, giving second-order global convergence for sufficiently smooth data and compatible boundaries.

An independent solution follows characteristics: \(u(x,t)=u_0(x-ct)\exp[-\int_0^t k(x-c(t-s))\,ds]\). For \(k=k_0+k_1\sin x\), the integral is \(k_0t+(k_1/c)[\cos(x-ct)-\cos x]\). Compare the split method first to a tightly integrated unsplit system on the same mesh, then to this continuum formula. Halve time at fixed mesh and halve the mesh at a suitably small time step. A single combined refinement cannot identify which contribution dominates.

How expensive is this factorisation?

On an \(m\)-by-\(m\) grid each half-step contains \(m\) tridiagonal systems of length \(m\). A tridiagonal factorisation and substitution lead to \(O(m^2)\) work per step. The small reference notebook deliberately uses the OneSimulation dense solver for each block of right-hand sides so the matrices can be inspected directly.

Tutorial on damping and noncommutation

Calculate the amplification of a strongly anisotropic mode

Work through the solution

With \(s\lambda_x=-10\) and \(s\lambda_y=-0.1\), the factors are \(-9/11\) and \(9/11\). Their product is \(-81/121\). The exact one-step decay is \(e^{-20.2}\), which is much smaller. Halve the time step until damping agrees with the resolution required.

Design a test where reversing the split should change nothing

Work through the solution

A constant reaction rate has \(k'=0\), so advection and reaction commute for periodic boundaries. With exact component updates the two orders agree at any time step. Change \(k\) to a sinusoid and use the order difference to expose the commutator.

LeVeque · diffusion and mixed time-dependent problems

Spatial discretisation and field equations · 7. Following the error from one reference triangle to an entire mesh

Derive a first-order energy error estimate and a second-order displacement estimate for a conforming linear triangular discretisation. Track the assumption on element shape at the point where it enters the proof.

Remove everything a linear element can reproduce

Let \(\widehat K\) be a fixed triangle and \(\widehat I\) its affine nodal interpolant. In two dimensions point evaluation is continuous on \(H^2(\widehat K)\), so \(\widehat I:H^2\to H^1\) is bounded. Choose an affine \(p\) to match the means of \(\nabla u\) and of \(u\) on the triangle. Apply the Poincaré inequality first to the two components of \(\nabla(u-p)\), then to \(u-p\). This bounds the whole \(H^2\) norm of \(u-p\) by a constant times the \(L^2\) norm of the second derivatives. Since \((I-\widehat I)p=0\),

\[|\widehat u-\widehat I\widehat u|_{H^1(\widehat K)} \le C|\widehat u|_{H^2(\widehat K)} .\]

This argument is the affine case of polynomial approximation by a seminorm. No constant in it depends on the eventual mesh. Choosing nodal values would require extra care for rougher solutions for which point evaluation is undefined.

Transform derivatives and area separately

Let \(F(\xi)=B\xi+b\) map the reference triangle onto \(K\). Physical gradients are \(B^{-T}\) times reference gradients. Pulling back second derivatives produces two factors of \(B\). The square-root determinant factors from integrating and undoing the map cancel in the resulting estimate:

\[|u-I_Ku|_{H^1(K)} \le C\|B^{-1}\|\,\|B\|^2\, |u|_{H^2(K)} .\]

A shape-regular family has uniformly bounded \(\|B\|\|B^{-1}\|\) and element diameter comparable to \(\|B\|\). Thus the right side is \(Ch_K|u|_{H^2(K)}\). For a triangle with legs \(h\) and \(h^2\) the general bound gives order one for its coefficient, since \(\|B^{-1}\|\simeq h^{-2}\). This shows where the guarantee is lost. Some thin triangles and specially oriented solutions still interpolate accurately.

Use Galerkin orthogonality to turn approximation into a PDE estimate

For homogeneous Dirichlet diffusion with \(0<\kappa_{\min}\le\kappa(x)\le\kappa_{\max}\), set \(a(v,w)=\int\kappa\nabla v\cdot\nabla w\). The conforming Galerkin solution satisfies \(a(u-u_h,v_h)=0\). Write \(e=u-u_h\). Then \(\|e\|_a^2=a(e,u-I_hu)\le\|e\|_a\|u-I_hu\|_a\). Summing squared local bounds gives \(\|e\|_a\le Ch\sqrt{\kappa_{\max}}|u|_{H^2}\). Here \(u\in H^2\), boundary conformity and accurate assembly are substantive assumptions.

For the mean-square error solve the dual problem \(a(v,z)=(v,e)\) with the same boundary conditions. If it satisfies the regularity estimate \(\|z\|_{H^2}\le C_{\rm reg}\|e\|_{L^2}\), then

\[\|e\|_{L^2}^2=a(e,z-I_hz)\le Ch\|e\|_a\|z\|_{H^2}.\]

Divide by \(\|e\|_{L^2}\) to obtain the extra power of \(h\). A reentrant corner or a coefficient interface can obstruct this regularity. Use the actual energy or output norm in the convergence study.

Why can a bad cell evade an affine patch test?

Every nonsingular triangle, including a very thin one, exactly reproduces an affine function. Such a patch tests assembly and transformation consistency. Curved test functions in several orientations are needed to expose geometry-dependent interpolation errors.

Tutorial on local and global accuracy

Compute the interpolation error on a right reference triangle

Work through the solution

For \(u=x^2\) on vertices \((0,0),(h,0),(0,h)\), \(I_Ku=h x\). The squared energy error for unit diffusion is \(\int_0^h(2x-h)^2(h-x)\,dx=h^4/6\). On a family of similar cells the local energy error scales like \(h^2\); the sum of squared errors over \(O(h^{-2})\) cells scales like \(h^2\).

Choose a mesh test for a collapsing angle

Work through the solution

Compare a uniformly refined mesh to one containing triangles with \(B\) singular values \(h,h^2\). Record the largest \(\|B\|\|B^{-1}\|\), solution and energy errors, and compare to the local scaled bounds. Growth of a bound alone does not prove a particular solution diverges. Rotate the test Hessian to probe whether the poor direction couples to curvature.

Download the executed OneSimulation notebook · Python source

The notebook reproduces the reference case and exposes the mesh or geometry parameters. Its saved output below comes from the actual OneSimulation solver.

"""Inspect shape-dependent interpolation error with a OneSimulation dense solve."""
import argparse
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import solve_dense


def experiment(base=0.5, ratio=0.25):
    if base <= 0 or ratio <= 0:
        raise ValueError("Base and altitude/base ratio must be positive")
    altitude = base * ratio
    vertices = np.array([[0., 0.], [base, 0.], [base/2, altitude]])
    # The affine interpolation coefficients reproduce the three nodal values.
    vandermonde = np.column_stack((np.ones(3), vertices))
    coefficients = solve_dense(vandermonde, vertices[:, 0]**2)
    expected = np.array([0., base, -base**2/(4*altitude)])
    np.testing.assert_allclose(coefficients, expected, atol=2e-14)
    gradient_error = np.sqrt(base*altitude/2 * (base**2/6 + base**4/(16*altitude**2)))
    print(f"Affine coefficients: {coefficients}")
    print(f"L2 norm of gradient error: {gradient_error:.8f}")

    barycentric = np.array([(i/30, j/30) for i in range(31) for j in range(31-i)])
    points = np.column_stack((base*(barycentric[:, 0]+barycentric[:, 1]/2), altitude*barycentric[:, 1]))
    exact = points[:, 0]**2
    linear = coefficients[0] + points @ coefficients[1:]
    fig = plt.figure(figsize=(10, 4.4), layout="constrained")
    ax = fig.add_subplot(121, projection="3d")
    ax.plot_trisurf(points[:, 0], points[:, 1], exact, color="#a6c8be", alpha=.8)
    for fraction in np.linspace(0, 1, 9):
        y = altitude*fraction
        x = np.linspace(base*fraction/2, base*(1-fraction/2), 40)
        ax.plot(x, np.full_like(x, y), coefficients[0]+coefficients[1]*x+coefficients[2]*y,
                color="#ad491d", lw=1)
    ax.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="y", zlabel="Scalar field u", title="Exact surface and affine interpolant")
    error = fig.add_subplot(122)
    ratios = np.geomspace(.02, 1, 100)
    error.loglog(ratios, base/(4*ratios), label="Spurious transverse gradient")
    error.scatter(ratio, abs(coefficients[2]), color="#ad491d")
    error.set(xlabel="Altitude / base", ylabel="Magnitude of transverse gradient")
    error.legend()
    fig.savefig("complete-numerics-triangle.png", dpi=170)
    return coefficients, gradient_error


if __name__ == "__main__":
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)
    parser.add_argument("--base", type=float, default=.5)
    parser.add_argument("--ratio", type=float, default=.25)
    args, _ = parser.parse_known_args()
    experiment(args.base, args.ratio)
    plt.show()

Affine coefficients: [ 0.   0.5 -0.5]
L2 norm of gradient error: 0.09547033

Spatial discretisation and field equations · 8. Which waves are allowed to enter a numerical boundary?

Diagonalise linear acoustics, impose one physical condition per incoming wave and compare diffusive and dispersive transport schemes on the same spatial Fourier mode.

Separate acoustic waves by their directions

For small perturbations in a uniform fluid, \(p_t+\rho c^2v_x=0,\ v_t+p_x/\rho=0\) with density \(\rho>0\), speed \(c>0\). Add and subtract \(\rho c\) times the second equation. The combinations \(w_\pm=p\pm\rho c v\) satisfy \(\partial_t w_\pm\pm c\,\partial_x w_\pm=0\). Reconstruct \(p=(w_++w_-)/2,\ v=(w_+-w_-)/(2\rho c)\). At the left boundary \(w_+\) enters and \(w_-\) leaves. These roles exchange at the right boundary.

For a rigid wall \(v=0\), set the incoming characteristic equal to the outgoing one. The reflected pressure has the same sign. At a pressure-release boundary \(p=0\), it has opposite sign. Prescribing \(p\) and \(v\) independently at the same boundary generally overspecifies a first-order system. For a diagonalizable hyperbolic matrix \(A=R\Lambda R^{-1}\) the same counting follows from \(R^{-1}u\), subject to independent boundary conditions on incoming components. Zero-speed or glancing modes require separate analysis.

Derive one amplitude and phase experiment for three methods

For scalar right-going \(u_t+a u_x=0\), set \(\nu=ah/\Delta x\). First-order upwind uses the upstream difference. Lax–Friedrichs replaces the central value by the average of its neighbours before taking the centred first derivative. Lax–Wendroff expands time to second order and uses \(u_{tt}=a^2u_{xx}\). With Fourier angle \(\theta=k\Delta x\), the three amplification factors are

\[G_U=1-\nu(1-e^{-i\theta}),\]
\[G_F=\cos\theta-i\nu\sin\theta,\quad\]

G_W=1-i\nu\sin\theta+\nu^2(\cos\theta-1).

For \(0\le\nu\le1\) these are stable in the periodic discrete \(L^2\) norm. For the last factor direct expansion gives \(|G_W|^2=1-4\nu^2(1-\nu^2)\sin^4(\theta/2)\). The exact factor \(e^{-i\nu\theta}\) has modulus one. At \(\nu=1/2,\theta=\pi/2\), upwind gives \(0.5-0.5i\), Lax–Friedrichs gives \(-0.5i\) and Lax–Wendroff gives \(0.75-0.5i\). Their magnitudes and arguments distinguish loss of contrast from arrival-time error.

Transfer the calculation to both members of the system

On a uniform grid update \(w_+\) by a right-going scheme and \(w_-\) by its mirrored left-going counterpart. For first-order upwind the outgoing stencil at either wall uses only interior data. Supply the incoming value using the physical relation. For centred and second-order stencils, ghost values or one-sided closures must also approximate derivatives at the boundary with a compatible accuracy and stability. A periodic amplification test alone cannot certify them.

Why can a second-order scheme create negative overshoots?

In Lax–Wendroff the downstream coefficient \((\nu^2-\nu)/2\) is negative for \(0<\nu<1\). At a step discontinuity the update can leave the interval spanned by neighbouring data. Its Fourier stability bound controls a norm and does not imply a maximum principle.

Tutorial on waves in an acoustic tube

Calculate one update next to a marked step

Work through the solution

Take \(\nu=1/2\) and \(u_{j-1}=u_j=0,\ u_{j+1}=1\). Upwind gives zero, Lax–Friedrichs gives \(1/4\), Lax–Wendroff gives \(-1/8\). The exact right-going step has not reached this upstream cell centre.

Infer the reflected wave from a non-ideal termination

Work through the solution

At the right boundary impose \(p=Zv\). Substituting the characteristic pair gives \(w_-=(Z-\rho c)w_+/(Z+\rho c)\). A matched termination \(Z=\rho c\) transmits the wave with zero reflection. Verify the rigid and pressure-release limits before using this relation in ghost cells.

Vector calculus through flux, circulation and singular sources

Electrostatic field theory and measurement

A closed-surface flux and a closed-path circulation answer different experimental questions. Starting from small physical collecting surfaces shows why the associated differential operators look different in Cartesian, cylindrical and spherical coordinates.

How a short displacement detects a derivative

A voltmeter measures a difference between endpoints. If a small displacement \(d\boldsymbol\ell\) changes a smooth potential by \(dV\), the gradient is the unique vector satisfying \(dV=\nabla V\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell\) for every displacement. Its component along an arbitrary unit vector is the slope in that direction. Its largest rate of increase occurs when the displacement is parallel to the gradient. A unit step in a coordinate label does not necessarily mean a unit physical displacement. This distinction is the source of the metric factors in curvilinear coordinates.

At position \((s,\phi,z)\) in cylindrical coordinates, the three physical components of displacement are \(ds,s\,d\phi,dz\). Equating the general dot product to \(\partial_s V\,ds+\partial_\phi V\,d\phi+\partial_z V\,dz\) gives

\[\nabla V=\hat{\mathbf s}\partial_sV+\hat{\boldsymbol\phi}s^{-1}\partial_\phi V+\hat{\mathbf z}\partial_zV .\]

The azimuthal factor converts a voltage change per radian into a voltage change per metre around a circle. For \(V=V_0(s/a)\cos\phi\) the radial and azimuthal components of \(-\nabla V\) are \(-V_0\cos\phi/a\) and \(+V_0\sin\phi/a\). Together they equal the constant Cartesian field \(-V_0\hat{\mathbf x}/a\), since \(s\cos\phi=x\). The apparent angular variation comes only from the rotating local unit vectors. Leaving out \(1/s\) would predict a nonuniform field and components with incompatible dimensions.

Count what leaves the sides of a shrinking cell

Consider a vector \(\mathbf F\), perhaps electric displacement measured in C/m². Define its divergence at a smooth point as the limiting outward flux per cell volume. In a Cartesian box \(\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z\), the opposing \(x\) faces give \([F_x(x+\Delta x)-F_x(x)]\Delta y\Delta z\). Dividing by volume and adding the other pairs gives \(\partial_xF_x+\partial_yF_y+\partial_zF_z\). The signed flux measures the local excess of outflow over inflow. A field can be large and uniform while its divergence is zero.

Repeat the count in a thin cylindrical wedge between \(s\) and \(s+ds\), angular width \(d\phi\), height \(dz\). The outer curved face has larger area than the inner one. Their net flux is \(\partial_s(sF_s)ds\,d\phi\,dz\). The two cuts at fixed angle each have physical area \(ds\,dz\) and differ by \(\partial_\phi F_\phi d\phi\). The axial faces contribute \(\partial_zF_z\,s\,ds\,d\phi\,dz\). Division by the wedge volume gives

\[\nabla\cdot\mathbf F=\frac1s\partial_s(sF_s)+\frac1s\partial_\phi F_\phi+\partial_z F_z .\]

A radial flow \(F_s=A/s\) has zero divergence at positive \(s\) because the same flux crosses every cylindrical side of fixed height. A constant radial magnitude instead has divergence \(A/s\): larger cylinders carry greater total flux and therefore enclose additional source. Specifying that a magnitude is constant says nothing about the divergence of its direction field.

In a spherical sector the radial areas grow as \(r^2\). For a spherically symmetric radial field \(F_r(r)\), the same count gives \(r^{-2}d(r^2F_r)/dr\). A sphere of radius \(r\) then has flux \(4\pi r^2F_r(r)\). Choosing \(F_r=Ar\) produces \(3A\) everywhere instead of \(A\); the two missing contributions in a one-dimensional derivative account for growth of transverse area. For a uniform charge density \(\rho\), setting \(3A=\rho/\epsilon_0\) immediately reconstructs the interior electrostatic field of a sphere.

Inspect a small loop before asserting that a potential exists

The component of curl normal to a surface is the limiting circulation of \(\mathbf F\) around an oriented small loop divided by its area. In an \(xy\) rectangle the upper side is traversed oppositely to the lower one. Opposite \(x\)-directed sides leave \(-\partial_yF_x\); opposite \(y\)-directed sides leave \(+\partial_xF_y\). Thus

\[(\nabla\times\mathbf F)_z=\partial_xF_y-\partial_yF_x.\]

For a planar polar loop the curved segments have different lengths, giving \((\nabla\times\mathbf F)_z=s^{-1}[\partial_s(sF_\phi)-\partial_\phi F_s]\). Curl and divergence use different faces and signs even when their cylindrical formulas share a factor \(1/s\).

Join many neighboring loops. Circulations on common edges cancel in pairs, leaving only the outer boundary; the summed area integrals of the curl give Stokes's theorem. A similar cancellation of internal face fluxes gives the divergence theorem. These are physical bookkeeping identities, and they motivate discretisations that attach oriented fluxes to shared faces and voltages to shared edges. Assigning two independent approximations to one shared face can spoil exact cancellation in a computed conservation budget.

Take \(\mathbf F=A\hat{\boldsymbol\phi}/s\) outside a thin excluded cylinder. Its ordinary curl is zero there. Any circle about the cylinder has circulation \(2\pi A\). A disk spanning this circle would pass through the excluded region, so a surface lying entirely inside the stated domain cannot supply the usual zero-curl conclusion. Locally \(\mathbf F=\nabla(A\phi)\) on any small patch avoiding a branch cut, while the putative potential changes by \(2\pi A\) after a full turn. This example explains the role of domain topology. A genuine static electrostatic source in ordinary free space has a globally consistent potential; a changing flux through an excluded region can instead generate precisely such an exterior circulation.

Measure curvature through neighboring voltages

Taking the divergence of a gradient defines \(\nabla^2V\). A Taylor expansion at a point gives

\[[V(x+h)-2V(x)+V(x-h)]/h^2=\partial_x^2V+O(h^2).\]

Adding the three Cartesian expressions relates the six neighbouring voltage readings to the local charge density. Equivalently, averaging a potential over directions at radius \(h\) removes all terms odd in the displacement. The angular mean of \(n_i n_j\) is \(\delta_{ij}/3\), so for smooth \(V\)

\[\langle V(\mathbf x+h\mathbf n)\rangle_\Omega=V(\mathbf x)+\frac{h^2}{6}\nabla^2V(\mathbf x)+O(h^4).\]

In a region with positive charge, Poisson's equation makes the spherical average smaller than the centre value. An uncharged saddle can have strong curvature along each of two axes that cancels in the sum. A local maximum along a single measurement line therefore does not prove that an interior source exists. To make such an inference one needs curvature in every independent spatial direction or independent symmetry information.

Near a boundary or dielectric interface this smooth Taylor analysis may break down. Enforce the integral flux balance on a cell straddling the interface instead. In media of different permittivity the normal derivative of \(V\) can jump even where there is no free charge. The physical differential operator is then \(\nabla\cdot(\epsilon\nabla V)\); simply inserting a pointwise averaged permittivity in front of a smooth Laplacian may erase the correct jump condition.

Use a test volume to give singular expressions meaning

Outside the origin the divergence of \(\hat{\mathbf r}/r^2\) is zero, yet every sphere centred there has flux \(4\pi\). To characterize its source measure the field against a smooth test function \(f\) of compact support. Excise a ball of radius \(\eta\), integrate by parts in the remaining exterior and note that its inward-facing inner boundary normal is \(-\hat{\mathbf r}\). The zero bulk divergence eliminates the interior term, leaving \(-\int(\hat{\mathbf r}/r^2)\cdot\nabla f\,dV\to4\pi f(0)\) as the ball shrinks. This is the defining action of the three-dimensional delta distribution:

\[\nabla\cdot(\hat{\mathbf r}/r^2)=4\pi\delta^3(\mathbf r),\]
\[\nabla^2(1/r)=-4\pi\delta^3(\mathbf r).\]

The factors and signs follow from an ordinary finite flux calculation. A delta distribution has no finite peak height to compare with a mesh value. A convergent field computation should reproduce its integral and its action on smooth functions. In spherical coordinates a radially concentrated charge can be represented with a radial delta only when the \(4\pi r^2\) integration weight and the convention for the endpoint are treated consistently. It is often safer to specify the enclosed charge directly.

For a finite regularisation take \(V_a=q/(4\pi\epsilon_0\sqrt{r^2+a^2})\). Differentiation gives

\[E_r=q r/[4\pi\epsilon_0(r^2+a^2)^{3/2}].\]

A spherical flux measurement encloses \(Q/q=r^3/(r^2+a^2)^{3/2}\). Its derivative divided by \(4\pi r^2\) gives

\[\rho_a=3qa^2/[4\pi(r^2+a^2)^{5/2}].\]

For every \(a>0\) the density is smooth; its peak scales as \(a^{-3}\), preserving a finite total charge. For fixed \(r>0\), \(Q/q\to1\) as \(a\to0\). Shrinking the collecting radius and the source core simultaneously at fixed \(r/a\) instead retains a nontrivial fraction. The resolution of the probe is part of the limiting experiment.

A sheet of dipoles produces a voltage jump without a net sheet charge

Replace an ideal neutral polarised layer by two parallel charge sheets, one at \(z=-d/2\) with density \(-\sigma\), another at \(z=+d/2\) with density \(+\sigma\). Assume both planes extend far beyond the detectors so their fields can be treated as infinite-plane fields. The field outside cancels. Between them it points from positive to negative,

\[E_z=-\sigma/\epsilon_0.\]

A voltmeter moved from below to above the pair rises by \(+\sigma d/\epsilon_0\). The result can stay finite when \(d\) shrinks provided that the dipole moment per area \(p_s=\sigma d\) is fixed. The fields away from the layer then converge to zero on both sides while the voltage difference persists. This model is useful for a surface dipole layer, for example when positive and negative charge distributions are displaced by molecular-scale distances.

It is essential not to infer the voltage jump from the net charge enclosed in a pillbox, since the net is zero for every \(d\). Write the signed density as \(\rho_d=\sigma[\delta(z-d/2)-\delta(z+d/2)]\). For any smooth test voltage \(f(z)\), integration gives \(\sigma[f(d/2)-f(-d/2)]\to p_sf'(0)\). The distribution with action \(f\mapsto p_s f'(0)\) is \(-p_s\delta'(z)\). In one dimension Poisson's equation is \(-\epsilon_0 V''=\rho\), so a solution is

\[V=(p_s/\epsilon_0)H(z)+{\rm constant},\]

where \(H\) is a step. Its derivative is a delta, its second derivative a delta derivative. Reversing the layer orientation reverses both the jump and the sign of this source distribution.

A conventional monopole sheet instead has \(\rho=\sigma_f\delta(z)\). Its potential is continuous and its normal derivative jumps. The two types of ideal source thus leave different signatures in electrode boundary models. For a voltage jump of 20 mV, the required \(p_s=\epsilon_0(0.020)=1.77\times10^{-13}\) C/m in vacuum. Spreading it across 1.0 nm requires \(\sigma=1.77\times10^{-4}\) C/m² and an internal field of 20 MV/m in that thin-layer model. The large internal field is consistent with a small voltage step because it acts only over one nanometre. At fixed \(p_s\) the field energy per area grows as \(p_s^2/(2\epsilon_0d)\). Thus taking a perfect zero-thickness dipole layer gives useful exterior fields and voltage jumps but not a finite internal energy. A physical model of finite layer thickness is necessary for that observable.

Moving a numerical grid across the sheet illustrates how to recognise the two cases. If its potential samples resolve a continuous cusp, integrating the inferred charge across one symmetric layer gives finite sheet charge. If instead the voltage contains a step, two neighbouring density bins can acquire large opposite entries whose sum tends to zero. Their first moment stays finite. A useful verification uses both \(\int \rho\,dz\) and \(\int z\rho\,dz\) and compares potential readings on each side. A code that preserves charge but smooths a dipole step into an incorrect zero voltage would pass the first test but fail the others.

An explicit flux audit on a nonuniform mesh

To separate geometrical signs from a particular physical equation, take a rectangular box \(0<x<L_x,\ 0<y<L_y,\ 0<z<L_z\) and an exactly known displacement \(\mathbf D=Axy\,\hat{\mathbf x}+B y^2\,\hat{\mathbf y}+Cz\,\hat{\mathbf z}\). The constants have units making each term C/m². Its divergence is \((A+2B)y+C\). Integrating over the volume gives total free charge \(L_x L_z[(A+2B)L_y^2/2+C L_y]\). At \(x=0,y=0,z=0\) the relevant normal components vanish. At \(x=L_x\), integrating over the face gives \(A L_x L_z L_y^2/2\). The \(y=L_y\) face gives \(B L_x L_zL_y^2\), and the top gives \(C L_xL_yL_z\). Addition reproduces the volume calculation, including its factors of two.

Choose numerical constants by specifying \(L_x=L_y=L_z=1\) m, \(A=2\) nC/m⁴, \(B=3\) nC/m⁴ and \(C=4\) nC/m³. The three positive-face contributions are 1, 3 and 4 nC, for total 8 nC. Split the volume at \(y=0.30\) m. The bottom subvolume has volume charge \(\int_0^{0.3}(8y+4)\,dy=1.56\) nC. Its new upper face carries \(B(0.30)^2=0.27\) nC. The bottom section of the \(x=L_x\) face carries \(0.090\) nC and that of the top carries \(1.20\) nC. Their sum is 1.56 nC. For the upper subvolume the same internal face must be counted with the opposite sign. Physical flux is single-valued on that shared face even if the cells on its sides have very different thicknesses.

Suppose a program evaluates the shared face once using a derivative from the lower cell and independently using another from the upper cell. A mismatch of 0.03 nC in those estimates generates a global charge defect when summing cells, although each side may display a locally plausible gradient. A conservative scheme stores one oriented face flux and uses the same number with opposite signs in both cells. Flux conservation can then hold to floating-point accuracy on any partition, without guaranteeing the shared flux is physically accurate. In our manufactured example a shared wrong value would move the two cell errors in opposite directions and leave the total exact. Compare the two individual subvolume charges as well as the global one.

Is this manufactured \(\mathbf D\) an electrostatic vacuum solution? In a homogeneous scalar dielectric, \(\nabla\times\mathbf D\) would have to vanish for a purely electrostatic field. Its \(z\) component here is \(-Ax\). Thus the displacement tests the divergence theorem and a face-flux data structure perfectly, but it does not simultaneously test a potential-gradient solver with constant permittivity. For the latter choose \(\mathbf D=-\epsilon\nabla V\) from an explicit potential before manufacturing the charge. This example separates validation of a mathematical operation from validation of a whole physical model, an important distinction when a beautifully closed flux budget is the only available diagnostic.

Reconstruct a field from its divergence and curl in a periodic measurement cell

Suppose a three-dimensional field map is tabulated on a periodic cube and you know two derived maps: its divergence \(d\) and its curl \(\mathbf w\). Can these determine the original field? Fourier analysis gives a practical construction and reveals the missing information. For a nonzero spatial wavevector \(\mathbf k\), use the convention \(\mathbf F(\mathbf x)=\sum_{\mathbf k}\mathbf F_{\mathbf k}e^{i\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf x}\). Then \(d_{\mathbf k}=i\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf F_{\mathbf k}\) and \(\mathbf w_{\mathbf k}=i\mathbf k\times\mathbf F_{\mathbf k}\). Decompose \(\mathbf F_{\mathbf k}\) into its component parallel to \(\mathbf k\) and the remaining transverse component. Elementary vector identities give

\[\mathbf F_{\mathbf k}=-\frac{i\mathbf k}{k^2}d_{\mathbf k}+\frac{i}{k^2}\mathbf k\times\mathbf w_{\mathbf k}\qquad(\mathbf k\ne0).\]

The first term supplies the requested divergence and zero curl. The second supplies the requested curl when \(\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf w_{\mathbf k}=0\), a compatibility requirement because the divergence of a curl vanishes. At \(\mathbf k=0\) both operations annihilate the constant field. Its mean must be measured independently. In a nonperiodic region suitable boundary data enter as well. This is a constructive version of the statement that divergence and curl determine a field only after specifying its domain, boundary information and any harmonic freedom.

For a concrete field take \(\mathbf F=(A\cos kx,B\sin kx,C)\). Its divergence is \(-kA\sin kx\), its only curl component is \(w_z=kB\cos kx\), and the constant offset \(C\hat{\mathbf z}\) drops out of both maps. The first input alone reconstructs the \(x\) component and the second the \(y\) component. A purely electrostatic solver may legitimately require \(B=0\) but will not recover \(C\) from the local charge distribution alone. A uniform applied field can be imposed by suitable distant electrodes. A strictly periodic scalar potential cannot represent a nonzero mean field without separately adding a linear background.

A grid-based reconstruction needs its own consistency check. A centred difference on a periodic grid maps \(e^{ikx}\) to \(i\sin(kh)e^{ikx}/h\), not exactly \(ik e^{ikx}\). A source plane wave at the highest alternating grid mode \(k=\pi/h\) has zero centred derivative at every node despite alternating sign. Treating discrete divergence measurements as exact continuum coefficients near that grid scale would infer falsely that an oscillatory field has vanished. Use a consistent discrete operator, stagger quantities if appropriate, and restrict quantitative interpretation to adequately sampled modes. The continuous Helmholtz identity is exact; the data acquisition and differentiation scheme decides which components of that identity are actually observable.

Enclose different amounts of a regularised source

Predict how the flux of a small sphere changes as it crosses the charge cloud. Compare the enclosed fraction with the local source density.

Tutorial · turn field values into reliable local measurements

Reconstruct a sheet from a voltage cusp

Two electrodes at \(z=\pm L\) are grounded. Inside the gap a measured potential is \(V_0(1-|z|/L)\). Find both fields and reconstruct the source, then explain what a centred finite difference reports at the cusp.

Work through the solution

For \(z<0\), \(E_z=-V_0/L\); for \(z>0\), \(E_z=+V_0/L\). The jump gives a sheet \(\sigma=2\epsilon_0V_0/L\) at zero. A centred estimate of the first derivative there returns zero by symmetry, reflecting the arithmetic mean of the two one-sided fields. It cannot be interpreted as either physical one-sided limit. The centred second difference is \(-2V_0/(Lh)\), so the inferred cell density grows as \(1/h\). Multiplying by a cell width \(h\) returns the finite sheet charge. This is the correct quantity to compare when refining the grid.

With \(V_0=2\) V and \(L=2\) mm the fields have magnitudes 1000 V/m and the sheet charge is \(17.7\) nC/m². Reversing \(V_0\) reverses both field vectors and the sheet sign without moving the cusp.

Can a voltage map distinguish a saddle from a charge?

Near the origin a section shows \(V(x,0,0)=V_c-Ax^2\), \(A>0\). Give a source-free three-dimensional extension and a second extension consistent with uniform positive charge. Propose a minimum additional measurement given no prior symmetry.

Work through the solution

The first is \(V_c-Ax^2+Ay^2\), independent of \(z\). Its Laplacian vanishes. The second is \(V_c-A(x^2+y^2+z^2)\), whose Laplacian is \(-6A\), giving \(\rho=6\epsilon_0A\). Both match the entire original line. Curvatures along \(y,z\) together with the given \(x\)-curvature determine the Laplacian. A small enclosing surface measuring normal electric flux provides the same information integrated over a volume and does not require numerical second differentiation. Neither a second derivative along one line nor a visibly crowded pair of voltage contours is a universal charge detector.

Further reading

  • Vary the width and collecting radius of a regularised charge
  • Fließbach, chapter 3 · distributions
  • Griffiths, 5th ed., chapter 1 · vector analysis and the delta function

When do boundary measurements determine a unique electrostatic field?

Electrostatic field theory and measurement

A boundary-value solver may converge while solving the wrong electrical experiment. Establishing which potentials, charges and fluxes are fixed gives a uniqueness proof and a way to turn an approximate field into an upper bound.

Specify the experiment before specifying the equation

An insulating region occupies a bounded volume \(\Omega\) with smooth conducting or insulating boundaries. A known free charge density \(\rho_f\) is embedded in the region. For a positive scalar linear permittivity \(\epsilon(\mathbf x)\), electrostatics reduces to

\[-\nabla\cdot(\epsilon\nabla V)=\rho_f.\]

To turn this into an experiment one must decide how each boundary is prepared.
